# 1. Business model and strategy

## Choosing a business model: dropshipping, high-ticket, FBA, private label, digital and affiliate

### What dropshipping is

- Dropshipping is a fulfillment model that lets you sell products without holding inventory. You outsource fulfillment to a supplier, which reduces upfront costs and minimizes financial risk compared with traditional retail or wholesale. [L0007.018]
- Dropshipping is a fulfillment method, not a separate business model. Research, selling and store building follow the same process as other Shopify e-commerce. [L0972.002]
- The model in this course is online-only, with no physical store locations. Each order is shipped by the supplier directly to the customer after the sale. [L0759.001]
- In a dropshipping model the store owner never buys or stores inventory. Each order placed on the store is forwarded to a supplier or fulfillment company, which picks, packs and ships the product straight to the customer. The store owner only builds the website, attracts buyers and passes on order details. [L0760.001]
- A dropshipping business rests on three parts: an online store where sales happen, supplier fulfillment that ships the orders, and traffic that brings buyers to the store. Each part must work before the business can make a profit. [L0759.002]
- Dropshipping is a modern version of retail: a store sells products made by other manufacturers, as department stores do. Being the middleman between manufacturer and customer is normal in retail and is accepted by customers. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0458.012]
- Dropshipping is only a fulfillment method, so the customer experience is largely the same as with any other brand. The supplier ships the order directly, and the store owner remains responsible for the sale. [L1251.006]
- With approved suppliers, the retailer holds no inventory for most products. Orders are placed with suppliers after each customer purchase, and the supplier ships directly to the customer. (2023.) [L1283.104]
- In a non-stocking dealer setup, the supplier keeps the products in stock and ships the orders to your customers, while your store handles marketing, order handling and customer service. You do not hold inventory in this model. [L0933.004]
- Dropshipping gives the seller no product control. You cannot change or design the product, and the supplier decides what is stocked and can remove a product at any time. Your job is marketing and customer service, and you can only suggest product changes to the supplier. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.007]

### High-ticket and low-ticket

- High-ticket dropshipping means selling higher-priced products online while a supplier ships them directly to the customer, so the seller does not hold stock. Beginners should confirm they understand this model before they search for products. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0913.002]
- High-ticket dropshipping combines a low startup risk (under $500 to start), an evergreen model, profit of $300 or more per sale, a sellable branded asset, a time to first profit of about 30 days, and skills that transfer to other online businesses. Low-ticket dropshipping of $10 to $100 items from China, or via eBay or Amazon, keeps low risk and transferable skills but lacks the other benefits. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0458.002]
- Dropshipping usually targets items above $200, and sometimes above $750 to $800, where the supplier ships and the store owner provides all customer service. Use this range for high-ticket products. [L1153.007]
- **Note on price thresholds:** the two sources use different cut-offs. The $10 to $100 range [L0458.002] describes low-ticket products, while the $200 and above range [L1153.007] describes the usual dropshipping target. Both are older or undated advice, so check current supplier prices before you set a product range. [L0458.002, L1153.007]

### How orders and payments flow

#### Basic cycle (older advice, 2021-22)

1. Open a store and attract customers to it. [L0458.003]
2. When a customer orders, place the same order with your approved supplier. [L0458.003]
3. The supplier ships directly to the customer, so you never hold inventory. [L0458.003]
4. Pay the supplier only after the customer has paid you. [L0458.003]
5. You manage the website and marketing while the supplier handles fulfillment. [L0458.003]

#### Full order flow (older advice, 2021-22)

1. The customer pays on your website by card or PayPal, and the charge is immediate. [L1480.001]
2. The payment provider sends the money automatically to your bank account. [L1480.001]
3. Only after you receive the customer's money do you place the order with your supplier. [L1480.001]
4. The supplier invoices you. [L1480.001]
5. You pay the supplier. [L1480.001]
6. The supplier ships the product directly to the customer, who receives it at home or at their business. The cycle repeats for each order, and repeat customers can be sold other items. [L1480.001]

#### Order flow with an app-based supplier (Source C)

1. The customer places an order on the online store, for example one built with Shopify. [L0760.002]
2. The store owner forwards the order information to a supplier or fulfillment company, typically through that company's app with a few clicks. [L0760.002]
3. The supplier picks the product from the manufacturer or stock, checks its quality, packs the order and ships it directly to the customer's door. [L0760.002]

Summary: the store is built, the customer orders, the order is passed to the supplier, and the supplier handles sourcing, packing and shipping. [L0760.002]

#### Payment timing and cash

- Payment flows first from the customer to the seller at the time of purchase. The seller then orders from the supplier and pays for the product afterwards, so no large stock is bought upfront. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1481.009]
- The payment gateways in use commonly pay out the sale amount on the same day, minus small fees. This means the seller has the money before paying the supplier. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1481.010]
- At the start, pay each supplier only after the customer's payment has been received, so no cash goes out before money comes in. Paying suppliers before the customer's funds reach your bank account is an option to consider later, once the business is more experienced and earns more. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.034]
- Dropshipping is self-funding: customer payments cover each supplier order, so profit can be taken at the end of each month. A physical product business requires fronting cash for inventory, so profit only comes after a difficult cash cycle is passed. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1154.014]

#### Who is involved

- The model has three players: the customer, you as the seller, and the supplier, with freight companies handling the physical delivery. It is simple in structure, but that does not make it easy to run. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.002]
- Marketing is the engine of the business. Without it, customers never find the website, so no sale or service can happen. High-ticket dropshipping is therefore effectively a marketing business that sells through a store. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.003]

### Benefits

#### Low capital and low risk

- Dropshipping has a low barrier to entry: no inventory is required, upfront costs and risk are reduced, no physical storefront is needed, and you can test new products and markets without large commitments. [L0007.019]
- Because no inventory is bought in advance, the seller avoids the risk of unsold stock and can spend the budget on marketing and selling instead of warehousing. [L0760.006]
- Dropshipping eliminates overstocking and understocking risk. You test products and niches without financial exposure, and you keep complete control over inventory levels. [L0007.021]
- Low-priced products are a lower-risk way to start dropshipping because they need little capital and no storage. Choosing items that ship directly from the supplier keeps inventory risk near zero. [L0345.001]
- Dropshipping is a low-risk way to gain business experience, since the main costs at the start are ad spend and the Shopify subscription. The real risk is wasted ad spend, so keep spend tied to measured results. [L0388.020]
- Dropshipping is commonly recommended as a starting point for beginners because it needs little money, a laptop and an internet connection, with no physical store, warehouse or upfront stock purchase. [L0760.009]

#### Speed, scale and flexibility

- Dropshipping enables quick scalability. You can expand product offerings rapidly, keep more control over operations, reduce warehouse and shipping overhead, and achieve better profit margins due to lower fixed costs. [L0007.020]
- Dropshipping enables rapid pivoting to market trends, the ability to expand or scale back product offerings quickly, and reduced financial risk from diversified offerings. This flexibility is a core competitive advantage. [L0007.022]
- Dropshipping allows a seller to switch products and suppliers easily, so many product ideas and niches can be tested without the cost of managing inventory. [L0760.007]
- A dropshipping store can list as many products as needed, which allows several offers in the same niche to be sold to existing and new customers. [L0760.011]
- Dropshipping eliminates the operational burden of inventory management and fulfillment. The capacity freed by outsourcing these functions can be directed to revenue-generating activities, such as marketing, customer acquisition and sales, which directly drive growth. [L0007.028]
- Dropshipping applies the lean startup logic: a seller tests an existing market product first, then customizes branding and holds inventory only after the product proves demand. [L0760.020]

#### Control and margin

- Selling on your own store avoids marketplace commissions, which allows higher prices or better margins than on platforms that take a cut of each sale. [L0760.008]
- Owning the storefront gives the seller control over marketing, pricing and all other business aspects, instead of being bound by a marketplace's rules. [L0760.012]
- Dropshipping removes warehousing and shipping overhead, so margin can be put into marketing rather than storage. The lesson presents this as a reason for higher profit margins than inventory-based models. [L0760.013]
- Dropshipping gives higher margins and more control, because you own the customer, can cut costs by negotiating with suppliers, and can run every ad type. (2023.) [L0855.076]
- On an own store the seller receives the customer data, which marketplaces typically keep. This data supports strategic decisions, community building and repeat sales. [L0760.026]

#### Skills and transfer

- Dropshipping is a starting point for acquiring skills and generating cash, and the same skills can open other businesses, such as a local marketing agency or a coaching business. [L1432.034]
- Once you can sell and market well, those skills transfer to agencies, coaching, consulting, online education, buying other e-commerce businesses, or building your own brand. The underlying sales and marketing concepts stay the same across products even when tactics change. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.008]

### Risks and limits

#### Limits of the dropshipping model

- You sell products that competitors also sell, so the product cannot be your differentiator. Better marketing and better customer service than competitors lead to more sales of the same products. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.004]
- Dropshipping is a real business that has existed for at least 15 years and is still viable, and it is cheaper to start than many other models. It still requires real marketing and real customer service. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.005]
- Dropshipping is a different proposition from selling your own brand. As a reseller you compete as one of many brands in a category, so relying on a single brand is risky, because if customers do not want it, nothing sells. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1456.033]
- Dropshipping from China is not a long-term, sustainable business model. Do not continue operating an unprofitable China-based store to recover investment. Instead, focus on brand dropshipping with US-based suppliers. [L0253.033]
- Dropshipping stores often fade after two or three months. Brands that last test consistently after a winning product and invest in customer experience. [L1213.071]
- Platforms such as Facebook do not ban dropshipping as a model. They judge account quality, customer ratings and copied content, so the way the store operates is what matters. [L1213.072]

#### Cash flow and ad spend

- Combine short-term cash flow with long-term recurring revenue. Cash from the first sale funds acquisition, while repeat revenue produces the profit. A churn-and-burn model depends on constant cash for traffic and is stressful. A long-game-only model suits high-ticket sales that rely on referrals and high margins. [L0378.004]
- The real risk in dropshipping is wasted ad spend, so keep spend tied to measured results. [L0388.020]

### Building your own brand versus dropshipping

#### Risks of starting a brand from scratch

- Starting a brand from scratch carries five major risks:
  1. High upfront investment in product development and inventory. [L0007.026]
  2. Difficulty choosing a product or niche with sufficient market demand. [L0007.026]
  3. Time-consuming process of building a website, sourcing materials and manufacturing. [L0007.026]
  4. Difficulty attracting customers and gaining traction in crowded markets. [L0007.026]
  5. Risk of overestimating demand and holding excess unsellable inventory. [L0007.026]
- Building a product and brand from scratch takes around four to six months from idea to product. It requires upfront investment in development and inventory, and it risks choosing a product with insufficient demand or overestimating sales and ending with excess stock. [L0760.016]

#### Lean startup approach

- The lean startup method (described in Eric Ries' book *The Lean Startup*) states that a business should validate the product and product-market fit before investing heavily in production or marketing, using a minimum viable product and a proof of concept. [L0760.019]

#### Brand over the long term

- A dropshipping brand takes longer to build, but it can produce steadier, more predictable income and allow the owner more time and stability. The trade-off is lower speed to launch and more upfront patience. [L1251.010]
- Running a store to scale a single product and then shutting it down is the disposable model, which limits long-term income. Plan the store as a multi-product brand from the start, even if it begins as dropshipping. [L1251.008]
- Dropshipping remained viable in 2023, but it needed more brand work to stay sustainable. Owners who treat the store as a long-term brand rather than a short-term sales vehicle are better positioned. [L1251.007]
- In the business model that sells other brands' products through your store, the store itself is the brand. It does not involve introducing your own private-label products. (Older advice, 2019.) [L1275.055]

### Compared with Amazon FBA and private label

#### Private label on Amazon

- With Amazon FBA you own the private label product, so you set the price and decide how much inventory to hold. Unlike dropshipping, you cannot blame a supplier when an item is out of stock, because the inventory risk is yours. [L1153.001]
- Amazon FBA handles storage, shipping, returns, refunds and most customer service for the seller. This lets one person or a small team scale a product without a large staff. [L1153.002]
- In FBA the seller's core jobs are product selection, finding a manufacturer, managing inventory levels, building a high-converting product page and driving traffic, often with Amazon ads. Inventory management is the seller's biggest responsibility, especially before Q4 (the holiday season). [L1153.003]
- One FBA product can reach about $10,000 profit per month, but this takes work and is not an overnight process. Most sellers become profitable within one to three months of starting, once the first inventory order is sold. [L1153.004]
- Compared with dropshipping, FBA sellers source and own their product, face a longer and trickier supplier phase, and depend on Amazon as a third-party platform. Overseas suppliers add time zone differences and a language barrier, so the seller must plan for both. [L1153.006]
- FBA private label products usually sit in a lower price range where impulse buying is strong, while dropshipping usually targets items above $200. [L1153.007]
- Amazon pays sellers directly into their bank account every two weeks in eligible countries, whatever bank the seller uses. The payout schedule supports cash flow planning for the business. [L1153.008]
- Case: a seller in the course group sold 430 units in two weeks, generating almost $9,500 in sales at an average price of $22 per unit. Over that span the sales data moved from 430 to 513 units, and the two weeks together brought in almost $10,000 in sales. This shows that a sub-$25 product can still produce solid revenue when the volume is consistent. [L1153.009]
- Case: a different seller sold 129 units in one week for $5,000 in sales at an average price of $43 per unit. Results like this show that a price around $40 to $45 can still generate strong revenue without needing a very high unit count. [L1153.010]
- Many new sellers aim to sell under $10, but this is a poor strategy for Amazon FBA. The course recommends choosing a product that can carry enough profit per unit to fund paid advertising. [L1153.011]
- Amazon FBA carries risks: high competition and saturation in popular categories, very thin price margins, Amazon controlling customer data and branding, a 20 to 30 percent commission on each sale, and risk of account suspension or restriction for policy violations. [L0760.014]
- Selling on Amazon requires bulk inventory and a higher risk. Margins can be as low as 15% or $5 to $10 per unit. Amazon keeps customer data and takes a cut of revenue, which makes building a brand harder than in dropshipping. [L0788.046]
- The 15% commission figure comes from a 2020 comparison, where marketplace commissions were the benchmark for Shopping Actions. Amazon was cited as charging 15% of the sale price, while the original Shopping Actions commission averaged about 12% and ranged from roughly 8% to above 12%. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0738.035]

**Note on Amazon commission:** the sources give 15% (2020 comparison) [L0738.035] and 20 to 30% (Source C) [L0760.014]. The 15% value is older. Check the current fee schedule before you set prices. [L0738.035, L0760.014]

#### Comparison summary

| Point | Dropshipping | Amazon FBA private label | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Inventory | Not bought in advance; supplier stocks or ships | Owned by the seller; seller carries the inventory risk | [L0760.006, L1153.001] |
| Supplier phase | Supplier ships directly to the customer | Longer and trickier; overseas time zone and language barrier | [L1153.006] |
| Price range | Usually items above $200, sometimes above $750 to $800 | Lower price range with strong impulse buying | [L1153.007] |
| Customer service | Store owner provides all customer service | Amazon handles most customer service | [L1153.007, L1153.002] |
| Time to profit | About 30 days to first profit (high-ticket, older) | Most sellers profitable within one to three months of the first inventory sale | [L0458.002, L1153.004] |
| Platform and data | Store owner owns the customer and data | Amazon controls customer data and branding | [L0760.026, L0760.014] |
| Commission | None on your own store | 20 to 30% (Source C); 15% (2020 comparison) | [L0760.008, L0760.014, L0738.035] |

### Digital products

#### Why digital products

- Digital products have no fulfillment costs and no shipping delays, and they can educate buyers. They are a separate business model that requires its own research on creation, advertising and finding products to sell. [L0502.042]
- Digital products such as ebooks give a gross margin of roughly 90 percent, because the product is created once and then sold repeatedly without extra cost per order. The only ongoing cost is traffic, paid or organic. [L0513.001]
- Digital products such as ebooks and digital planners carry no fulfillment cost. This makes them profitable at low margins while still delivering the product instantly. [L1184.062]
- **Note on digital margins:** one source gives a gross margin of roughly 90% for ebooks [L0513.001], while another describes them as profitable at low margins because there is no fulfillment cost [L1184.062]. Both apply: the first describes the margin on each sale, the second describes the profitability of low-priced items. [L0513.001, L1184.062]
- Digital products have instant fulfillment. The buyer receives a download link or the PDF by email right after purchase, so there are no shipping delays or lost-parcel problems. Because fulfillment is easy and customers get what they expect, customer support load and refund impact are smaller than with physical shipped products. [L0513.002]
- Digital downloads let a seller monetize traffic from countries the physical product cannot ship to, and designs with no written instructions avoid a language barrier. Use digital offers to capture buyers that a physical-only product would lose. [L1153.081]
- Market saturation is not a real concern for knowledge products, because each new author can add value through a different point of view on the same topic. [L0513.003]
- Digital products can run alongside physical dropshipping products in the same business, and copywriting skills transfer to both. [L0513.004]
- When selling the same digital product in another country, adjust the pricing, the payment providers and the wording to that market. Expertise in how one country differs from another, such as its payment setup and local terms, is itself a value that can be turned into an ebook. [L0513.047]
- Digital products such as Lightroom presets sold well because many people lack the skill or time to edit photos themselves and buy presets to adjust. Choose digital products that solve a clear problem for the buyer. [L1214.020]

#### Product size rules

- A recipe ebook should not be sold below 20 pages. Other digital products can be shorter, for example a two-page Facebook ads strategy, as long as the buyer gets the full value they need. [L1131.038]

#### Starting with an information product

- A first online venture can be a low-cost info product, such as a self-published e-book, used mainly to learn how online sales work. Asking an established operator what they sell and how they market it can lead to a more proven model, such as dropshipping. [L0863.014]

### Affiliate marketing

#### Model and types

- In affiliate marketing you promote a product you neither create nor fulfill, and you earn a commission when a visitor you referred buys it. For digital products sold through affiliate directories such as ClickBank, the merchant handles refunds and customer questions. [L0514.001]
- A personal brand built on consistent content, such as a blog, podcast or regular guest posts, creates credibility and a loyal audience that follows you across products. It is a long-term model with a slow start and more time commitment. Once established, you can keep promoting new products in the niche. [L0514.002]
- A funnel-based affiliate business has no personal face and builds a weaker audience connection, but it launches faster. You can set up several niche sites, most of the work is front-loaded, and afterward the sites run largely on autopilot with reduced maintenance. [L0514.003]
- Digital affiliate offers include downloads, software subscriptions such as website builders and email autoresponders, and non-online offers such as cruises. Choosing among these depends on what your audience already searches for and wants to buy. [L0514.005]

### Print on demand

- Print on demand is very hard to make meaningful money with, because profit per sale is tiny. It is better treated as a brand-building add-on than as a profit source, especially since personal items such as clothing and bedding have high return rates when fit or feel does not match expectations. [L1097.005]
- A brand merchandise store that sells inexpensive print-on-demand items such as t-shirts, mugs and hats has very thin margins. Keep it for brand merchandise rather than treating it as a profit source. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0852.113]
- Print on demand is a community-building add-on, not a primary revenue source. Add it only after a store has a loyal audience that wants branded products. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0961.078]
- Print on demand is not recommended, because product prices are too low, margins are too thin, ads are hard to run profitably, and there is too little profit per sale for the work involved. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0967.089]

**Note on print on demand:** the 2020 sources differ in strength. One treats it as merchandise only [L0852.113], one as a community add-on [L0961.078], and one as not recommended [L0967.089]. The 2025 source gives the most current position: a brand-building add-on, not a profit source [L1097.005]. [L0852.113, L0961.078, L0967.089, L1097.005]

### Marketplaces

#### Marketplace selling as a separate model

- Marketplace selling is a separate model from dropshipping. The seller lists products on large retail marketplaces without stocking them, and fulfills an order only once it sells. (Older advice, 2019.) [L0663.029]
- Etsy suits small handmade or home businesses and is less suited for building a branded store than Shopify, which gives more control over brand presentation. [L0561.050]
- Depending on a single marketplace is a single point of failure: an account suspension or restriction for a policy violation can shut the whole business down. A seller who runs their own website keeps the business alive regardless of what any one marketplace decides. [L0760.025]
- Marketplaces such as Amazon can remove a seller's account and destroy the business overnight. An own website gives control over the brand, the customer data and the option to sell the business later. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0771.006]

### Is dropshipping still viable?

- Dropshipping is not dead. Claims that it has died come up every year, while e-commerce keeps growing. It would only die if people stopped buying online. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.006]
- Dropshipping is a real business that has existed for at least 15 years and is still viable. It is cheaper to start than many other models. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1480.005]
- Dropshipping is a short-term model only when done poorly. Done correctly, it builds a brand and customer knowledge that can be used later to sell your own products. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1481.025]
- Dropshipping stores often fade after two or three months. Brands that last test consistently after a winning product and invest in customer experience. [L1213.071]
- Platforms such as Facebook do not ban dropshipping as a model. They judge account quality, customer ratings and copied content. [L1213.072]
- After the pandemic, e-commerce adoption normalized to the historical 20-year growth trend. The spike during lockdown created excess capacity. Shopify laid off 1,000 people (10% of its workforce) on July 26, 2022, because growth returned to normal rather than to a sustained high trajectory. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0272.055]
- Shopify's public data reportedly showed about a 10% revenue increase for the average store in Q1 2025 compared with Q1 2024. Use this only as a general market signal. [L0972.003]
- AI dropshipping keeps the same business model as regular dropshipping: selling products online without holding inventory. The difference is that AI tools are applied at every step of the process, so the seller can move faster, smarter and leaner. [L1459.001]
- Between 2016 and 2018, vendors flooded AliExpress listings with lengthy delivery times. The approach paid off because Facebook advertising was inexpensive, yet the buyer experience suffered. [L1459.003]
- Branded dropshipping took off from 2019 through 2021. Quicker delivery, stronger ad creatives and bespoke storefronts distinguished the successful operators from the rest, but most workflows stayed hands-on and experimentation moved slowly. [L1459.004]
- From 2023 onward, the AI era allows a business to run with fewer people, lower costs and data-driven decisions instead of intuition alone. [L1459.005]
- **Note on viability:** the sources do not conflict on whether the model works. "Not dead" [L1480.006] applies to dropshipping as a whole, while "fades after two or three months" [L1213.071] applies to stores run without a brand plan. The short-term outcome depends on how the store is built. [L1480.006, L1213.071, L1481.025]

### Choosing a business model

- When choosing an online business model, compare the options on three criteria: whether it can be run remotely, how much startup capital it needs, and whether it can be built part-time next to a job. Common options to compare are affiliate marketing, selling a service, selling online education and selling physical products through e-commerce. Physical-product dropshipping can meet all three criteria for someone who still has a job and fixed financial obligations. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1014.019]
- Dropshipping with approved suppliers and low-priced products suits a first step with low capital. Choose the model that matches your remote working needs, your starting capital and the time you can give alongside other obligations. [L1014.019, L0345.001, L1283.104]

## High-ticket dropshipping model and unit economics

### Why high-ticket products: the core economic argument

- Products at $200 and above support full-time income in 15-30 minutes a day, because they need fewer sales to reach profit targets, generate fewer support issues, and earn more profit per order. [L0012.002]
- Each order carries more gross profit, so fewer orders reach a revenue target. The trade-off is higher ad cost and higher conversion risk per sale. [L0546.001]
- Marketing a $3,000 product costs about the same as marketing a $300 product, so profit per marketing dollar is much better on high-ticket items. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0458.005]
- Higher-priced products earn more profit per sale, so a store reaches the same profit with fewer customers. Low-ticket products still sell but need far more orders to match the same earnings. [L0964.156]
- Favour fewer customers who each spend a meaningful amount over high-volume, low-price sales. Thousands of small orders multiply support work, returns and complaints. (older advice, 2021) [L0247.003]
- Low-ticket products such as phone cases sell easily and ship cheaply, but a store built on them needs very high order volume, tends to leave little profit and can cause burnout. High-ticket products are the preferred choice for drop shipping because fewer sales are needed for the same profit. [L0474.002]
- High-ticket dropshipping sells products around $1,000 or more instead of cheap goods from overseas marketplaces. Each sale brings in enough gross profit to earn a meaningful income without large sales volume. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.001]
- Cheap dropshipping of goods shipped from China via social media has low product quality, shipping times of 3 to 4 weeks, razor-thin profit per order and a constant need for new winning products. Sales can collapse overnight when no new winner is found. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0771.003]
- Cheap dropshipping with 3 to 4 week shipping leads to angry customers, broken or low-quality goods and high ad spend. High-ticket dropshipping with local suppliers avoids most of these problems. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.003]
- Low-ticket items such as 40 USD products create many small orders, thin margins and fulfilment problems. Avoid them in high-ticket dropshipping, even though low-ticket dropshipping is marketed heavily. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0771.062]
- The model is the same as traditional retail that stocks several brands. The store sits between manufacturer and customer, which shoppers accept as normal; being a middleman is not a weakness of the model. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0771.009]
- The model is presented as more stable than low-ticket dropshipping because a business can earn meaningful income from a moderate number of large orders, without the very high order volume that low-margin products depend on. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.029]

### Profit per order and order volume

- Orders needed to reach a target profit = target profit ÷ net profit per order. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0771.004]
  - Example: a 40 USD item with about 10 USD net profit per order needs 120 orders to match the 1,200 USD net profit of one 6,000 USD infrared sauna.
  - Five saunas (6,000 USD profit) equal 600 orders of the 40 USD item. [L0771.004]
- Units needed per month = target monthly profit ÷ profit per unit sold. [L1147.002]
  - Example: a $3,000 target profit ÷ $3 profit per unit = 1,000 units; ÷ $300 profit per unit = 10 units. [L1147.002]
- Example: a $10 accessory sold at a 30% margin earns $3 per order. A $1,000 item at the same margin earns $300 per order. The cheap item needs 1,000 orders a month to reach $3,000 profit; the expensive item needs 10. [L1147.004]
- Choose products so the fewest possible orders produce the target profit, because each order brings support, processing and error work. For a lean, low-maintenance store, profit per sale matters more than sales count. [L1147.001]
- Counting order volume as success is misleading. A store with many orders earning a few dollars each can still fail to produce meaningful profit. Measure net profit per order and total net profit. [L1147.005]
- Sauna comparison: an infrared sauna at about $6,000 with roughly $1,200 profit per order compares with eyelash sets at $40 with about $10 profit per order. Matching one sauna requires 120 eyelash orders. Five saunas a day equals 720 eyelash orders, which needs a large team and leaves no lifestyle benefit. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0458.004]
- Conflict on the five-sauna figure: [L0458.004] gives 720 eyelash orders, but its own numbers give 5 × 120 = 600, which is the figure in [L0771.004]. Use 600 for this calculation; 720 does not follow from the stated figures. [L0458.004, L0771.004]
- Example store: 30 orders per month at an average order value of $1,000 generates $30,000 in sales, about one sale per day. In the example this supported $5,000 of monthly net profit. (older advice, 2019) [L1002.003]
- Typical e-commerce products sell for around $50, so a high-ticket store handles much lower order volume than a standard store at similar revenue. Plan staff and processes for fewer, larger orders. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0949.062]
- On a cheap dropshipping product sold for $50, profit after all costs can be $10 or less, so high volume is needed to earn real money. Gross margin in percentage terms can be similar to high-ticket, but the absolute profit per sale is far lower. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.004]

### Price thresholds and product selection

- Sources give different price thresholds. Use each one for the purpose stated:
  - Category definition: a product counts as high ticket when its average order value is above $200. Some high-ticket stores average close to $1,000, others reach several thousand dollars. (older advice, 2021) [L0469.003]
  - Category definition: products at $200 and above are high ticket. [L0012.002]
  - Store-level minimum (Source B's program): products with an average selling price of at least $800, with no maximum price, sourced from brands or suppliers that already sell in the customer's own country. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0458.001]
  - Store-level minimum: individual items below $800 can stay in the range, but the average across the store must reach 800 USD or more. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0771.001]
  - Typical product price: high-ticket products typically sell for USD 1,000 to 2,000 or more. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0781.002]
  - Target for a single product: aim for average selling prices of about $1,000 or more. The example product retails near $3,000 and is bought from the supplier for about $2,000. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.002]
- Starting price levels from 2019: about 200 to 1,000 USD per product when there is little phone support. Higher levels such as 3,000 USD are workable if the owner answers customer phone calls. (older advice, 2019) [L0356.009]
- High-ticket dropshipping needs a narrower product and customer match than standard dropshipping. The product must justify a higher price, and customers must be in a position to buy higher-priced items. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1058.003]
- Lower-ticket products such as glassware are hard to scale in a lifestyle business. Do not mix them with high-ticket items, but they can be promoted as affiliate content, where you earn commissions by linking to other sellers' products or services. (older advice, 2020) [L0358.051]
- Whether a niche is profitable depends on the supplier's markup and shipping cost per product, not on the niche name. (older advice, 2021) [L0834.001]

### Supplier model: drop shipping, MAP and branded supply

- In drop shipping, the store holds no stock. The owner buys from the supplier only after a customer orders, the supplier ships directly to the customer, and the owner runs the website, marketing and customer contact. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0771.002]
- The store sells the supplier's products without holding inventory: the supplier ships the order after the store sends it. Profit depends on the spread between MAP and wholesale after shipping, not on stock. (older advice, 2022) [L0854.111]
- Drop shipping is worth doing only when the gap between MAP and wholesale is large enough to pay for free shipping, ad spend and a profit. (older advice, 2021) [L0834.001]
- High-ticket dropshipping works through the operator's own store. Each supplier must approve the store before it can sell that supplier's products. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0912.001]
- The operator runs their own website and customer service. The store carries its own product range, which must meet the program's criteria and specifications. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0438.015]
- A legitimate model partners with brands that want a sales channel, sells at a markup set by the brand's MAP pricing, and focuses on accurate product information and a good buying experience. Avoid misrepresenting products or overselling features. (2025) [L0807.046]
- Example: a $2,000 sofa with a MAP of $2,000 is sold at that price, with delivery time, included warranty and product details shown on the page, and shipped within two to three days. The buyer chooses the product because they want it, and the store attracts them only with accurate information. (2025) [L0807.047]

### B2B and B2C

- The pool splits into B2B equipment categories (commercial kitchen, lab, industrial, gym-facility and amusement items) and consumer categories. [L0546.003]
- B2B buyers need a different sales approach, longer decision cycles and stronger trust signals than consumers. [L0546.003]

### Sales process and customer contact

- Very high-priced products in a niche typically require pre-selling (collecting payment before fulfilment) and direct phone calls with customers. Lower high-ticket products are more hands-off and need less customer interaction. [L0045.008]
- Decide your comfort level with phone sales before selecting an expensive niche, because the price point directly determines how much customer contact you will manage. [L0045.008]
- Higher-priced products usually need more pre-sale and post-sale interaction with buyers. (older advice, 2021) [L0469.003]
- High-ticket products have a longer sales process, with several calls and contacts before the customer buys. Track every unconverted prospect and automate follow-up with abandoned cart emails where possible. [L1000.052]

### Workload and operations

- Workload at scale: at roughly 10,000 to 30,000 USD monthly sales the work can be kept low. At roughly 80,000 to 100,000 USD monthly sales the workload roughly doubles because of calls, emails, shipping issues, returns, maintenance and advertising. [L1122.004]
- Low-volume, high-ticket products reduce the need for staff, multiple suppliers, phone support and live chat. High-volume, low-ticket stores often require a team to process orders and handle customer contact. [L1147.007]
- Fewer orders leave less room for fulfilment errors such as wrong items or damaged shipments, which keeps the business easier to run as a lifestyle business. [L1147.008]
- Because profit per sale is high, a high-ticket business can be run by one owner or with one or two helpers. A virtual assistant can handle phone calls, instead of the large team that low-ticket order volume needs. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.005]
- High-ticket dropshipping has low overheads because no large stock is bought upfront, and the seller can build the website alone with step-by-step guidance. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.024]
- Fewer customers who each spend a meaningful amount means fewer support tickets, returns and complaints than a high-volume store. (older advice, 2021) [L0247.003]

### Launch conditions and build pace

- The upfront cost is roughly USD 100 to 500 to reach launch. No warehouse, inventory stock, real estate or significant equipment is required. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0438.006]
- Compared with most other business types, the upfront financial commitment is comparatively low, and the business can produce steady cash flow once it runs. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0912.002]
- The low entry cost means the business can be started while still working a job, which suits people with fixed financial obligations. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1014.005]
- High-ticket products should be launched only after the brand is established in the industry: the name is known, many relationships exist and a sales track record is in place. This groundwork pays off over the long term. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0768.002]
- Launching a big-ticket product requires enough cashflow and enough brand strength to support it. Do not start without both, and invest the time needed to do it properly. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0768.020]
- The build is slower: high-ticket sells fewer units to fewer customers than low-price e-commerce, so building a large customer base takes longer. Plan for this slower build and look for ways to offset the downside. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0412.002]

### Revenue scale and expectations

- In this framework, a high-ticket dropshipping business should already operate through a standalone website and generate roughly $1 to $10 million in annual revenue. The system is not meant for businesses below that range or for pre-launch businesses. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0414.001]
- High-ticket dropshipping businesses can reach eight-figure annual revenue, meaning $10 million or more per year. This is an upper-end outcome, not a typical first-year result. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0912.004]
- Wayfair (US) and Temple & Webster (Australia) are cited as large online retailers that began as high-ticket dropshipping businesses. Their current revenue figures were not verified in the lesson. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.028]

### Who the model fits

- The optimal model sells quality products at fair prices exclusively to upper-middle-class customers. This positioning reduces customer service intensity and operational friction, and lets the business run with minimal owner involvement, which is the core requirement for profitable scaling. [L0019.005]
- The model suits operators who want a lean store with few orders, few staff and low maintenance, and who value profit per sale over sales count. [L1147.001, L1147.007]
- The model suits operators who can start while working a job, and who have fixed financial obligations that need a low entry cost. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1014.005]
- The model suits operators who are comfortable with phone sales and pre-selling when the niche is very expensive. [L0045.008]
- The model suits operators who can run it alone or with one or two helpers and a virtual assistant for calls. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1481.005]

### Who the model does not fit

- Pre-launch businesses and businesses below roughly $1 to $10 million in annual revenue are outside this framework. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0414.001]
- Operators without the cashflow or brand strength to support a big-ticket launch should not start. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0768.020]
- Operators who have not yet established the brand, relationships and sales track record should not launch high-ticket products. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0768.002]
- Operators who expect passive income. High-ticket dropshipping is still real work. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0912.001]
- Operators who are not willing to make phone calls or pre-sell very expensive items should avoid the most expensive niches. [L0045.008]
- Operators who need a fast-growing customer base. The build is slower than in low-price e-commerce. (older advice, 2021-22) [L0412.002]
- Operators who depend on high order volume of cheap products. Low-ticket items create many small orders, thin margins and burnout, and should be avoided in this model. [L0474.002, L0771.062]
- Operators who want to sell lower-ticket items in the same store. Mixing them with high-ticket items is not recommended; use affiliate content for them instead. (older advice, 2020) [L0358.051]
- Operators whose customers cannot buy higher-priced items. The product must justify the price, and customers must be able to pay it. (older advice, 2021-22) [L1058.003]

## Where to operate: country, market and legal entity

### Choosing the home or target market

#### Principles

- Choose markets by buying power, not by cheap traffic. A country where people have little money to spend is a poor place to test a product. A market where people buy a lot online, especially cheap items with fast delivery, is a good candidate. [L0361.052]
- Dropshipping can run in a country with an established e-commerce market, such as the US, UK, Canada, Australia, New Zealand or European countries. Where e-commerce is less developed, you can still run the business in the US from abroad, but this adds setup steps and costs. The US is the easiest large market for this. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0458.011]
- The model works only in a country with an established e-commerce market where Google Shopping is running. In countries with underdeveloped e-commerce, the local store may not work yet. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0771.010]
- To check whether your country has a real domestic e-commerce market, ask whether you regularly buy online from websites based in your own country. If you do, local suppliers and payment options probably exist. If you mostly buy from overseas sites, a store aimed at local customers will be harder to run. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1405.036]
- Entering a new country should follow research of that market, not assumptions. Countries such as Australia and the UK were once advised against but later proved successful, so a country like Spain can be a good market if your own research supports it. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1068.008]
- The country-specific demand test is an addition to the core product research method, not a replacement. Every other part of the core research method still applies when you target a foreign market. [L1072.012]
- A core dropshipping blueprint can be run from any country. International sellers should keep most of their effort on the core process and treat country-specific adjustments as small add-ons, not a separate model. [L0587.001]
- Choose the countries you advertise to before you set prices. The main markets at the time were the US, UK, Australia, New Zealand and Canada. Each market needs its own price in local currency, because a price that looks normal in one currency can look far too cheap or expensive in another. [L0999.070]
- The local currency for the 800 price minimum is the currency of the country you operate from. If you are based in a country without an established e-commerce market, the reference is USD and the business is set up in the US. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0994.029]

#### Default rules by situation

| Your situation | Default rule | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Home country has an established e-commerce market | Run the first high-ticket business there, with local suppliers, products stocked in local warehouses and local customers. | L0781.007, L0781.006, L0781.013 |
| Home country has no established e-commerce market | Do not start the business there. Default to the US market or the market of your citizenship country, if that market is workable for you. | L0781.008, L0781.009, L1405.004 |
| Residents of Australia | Start with an Australian business that sells to Australians through Australian suppliers. Australia is hard for non-residents. | L1405.002, L0771.011, L1405.005 |
| Residents of the UK | Sell in the UK. UK-only sourcing is usually the simpler starting point. | L1405.002, L0781.015 |
| Residents of Canada | Sell in Canada, in the US, or in both. Sources differ on the order, see the Canada notes below. | L1405.003 |
| Non-citizens and overseas sellers | The US is the default. It is the easiest place for a non-citizen to form a business. | L1405.004, L1405.005 |

*(Rows 1-3 and 5 are older, 2021-22.)*

#### Home-country default in detail

- For a first high-ticket business, the best default is to run it from the country where you currently live. This keeps shipping, customs and currency complexity low. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.006]
- If your country has an established e-commerce market, you can run high-ticket dropshipping there. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.007]
- If you live in a country without an established e-commerce market, do not start the business there. Choose a different target market instead. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.008]
- When your country has no e-commerce market, the default alternative is the US market or the market of the country where you hold citizenship. The second option only applies if that market is workable for you. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.009]
- For a first store, sell in the country where you live if that country has a real domestic e-commerce market. Local suppliers, local currency and local payment accounts avoid cross-border payment problems. Expansion into other countries can wait until the first business runs well. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1405.001]
- For a first store, a market in the owner's home country can keep the business simpler when enough suppliers there will sell to dropshippers. Cross-border sourcing from a neighbouring country is a workable alternative, but it adds currency and shipping questions to the pricing. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0905.027]
- A business can be run across borders, with the business registered in one country while the operator lives in another. Partnerships with owners in different countries, such as Australia and the US, are also possible. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1014.006]
- Example: a first store in home lighting (chandeliers, pendant lights and wall lights) sourced its products in the same country and sold to local customers. Starting with domestic sourcing and domestic buyers is a simple way to launch a first store in a single market. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1014.007]

#### Step-by-step: choosing a first market (high-ticket, Source B method)

1. Determine the country where you currently live. [L0781.013]
2. Check whether that country has an existing e-commerce market. [L0781.013]
3. If it does, run your first high-ticket business there with local suppliers, products stocked in local warehouses and local customers. [L0781.013]
4. If it does not, use the US market or the market of your citizenship country as your default target. [L0781.013]

*(Older, 2021-22.)*

Examples for this method:

- A citizen of one country who lives abroad in a market without e-commerce can choose between a business in their home country's market and a business in the US market. For instance, an Australian living in Thailand could run either an Australian-market or a US-market business. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.011]
- Thailand and Singapore are cited as places where residents often lack an established e-commerce market. Someone living there would default to the US market or their citizenship country's market rather than the local one. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.014]
- A UK-based high-ticket seller can source from UK suppliers for UK customers, or possibly from European suppliers. Brexit makes the European sourcing option harder, so UK-only sourcing is usually the simpler starting point. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.015]

#### Country notes

**United States**

- Focus primarily on the US market because it is larger. *(Older, 2019.)* [L0356.011]
- The United States produced the best results among the big markets tested, so the focus was placed there. Test countries one at a time and keep the strongest market as the base. [L0694.033]
- The US is the easiest large market for a foreign operator. Australia is hard for non-residents. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0771.011]

**Canada**

Sources differ on how to combine Canada and the US. Use the version that matches your situation:

- Source B (2021-22): Canadian residents can sell in Canada, in the US, or in both, since the US is geographically close and easier to reach from Canada. *(Older.)* [L1405.003]
- Older Source A advice (2018-2019): make the US the main market and sell to Canada as a secondary market. *(Older.)* [L1085.081, L0356.011]
- Older Source A advice (2019): Canadian sellers usually get better results by drop shipping to both the US and Canada rather than only to Canada. *(Older.)* [L1068.009]
- A 2025 unit: a store can sell only to Canada, or sell to the US from Canada, and both approaches have worked for operators. The US market was described as more than 10 times larger than Canada's. [L1097.003]
- Selling into the US from Canada can be harder because heavy items cost more to ship across the border. Many suppliers with US warehouses are therefore chosen for a Canadian business. A US EIN is recommended for doing business in the US. [L0360.084]

**United Kingdom**

- The UK market can be started as easily as the US if the owner knows that market better, since members there do similarly well and the time zone is the same. Price research should follow the chosen market. [L0941.006]
- A UK-based seller can source from UK suppliers for UK customers. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.015]

**Australia and New Zealand**

- Australia is hard for non-residents. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1405.005]
- Countries such as Australia were once advised against but later proved successful. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1068.008]
- After its home market, a brand expanded into Canada and Australia. For each new country it looked for local micro-influencers and fashion bloggers to build sales there. [L0790.101]

**Europe**

- EU markets are easier for a beginner to enter than the US, Canada, UK and Australia, because CPMs are much lower and competition is lower. The top four English-speaking markets carry high CPMs, which makes an unproven single store hard to launch there. [L0660.005]
- For a German-speaking seller, the expansion order is: test the US or Canada, then Australia and New Zealand. If the product fails there, test Germany, Switzerland and Austria, where you can communicate fluently with customers. [L0549.013]
- Expanding from North America into the EU is often impractical, because most suppliers have no EU warehouses and shipping can cost around ten times the North American rate. Expansion makes more sense when a neighboring market can be served from existing supply, such as moving from Spain to France. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.009]

**Asia**

- The author advises against launching dropshipping into Asian markets such as Thailand for now, because the market is less proven and needs more effort. Local marketplaces such as Lazada show that some companies are willing to take that pioneer risk. Whether to enter depends on goals, risk tolerance and available time. [L0910.003]

### Cross-border shipping, sourcing and pricing

- High-ticket dropshipping is generally a domestic business, because sellers rarely ship products from their home country across borders. Some regions, such as parts of Europe, are exceptions. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.001]
- Cross-border shipping of high-ticket products adds cost through bulkier packages, higher freight rates, customs duties and currency conversion. These costs can significantly raise the price for either the seller or the buyer. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.003]
- Launching a cross-border high-ticket model without pricing in freight, customs duties and currency conversion can leave little or no margin. Check these costs before choosing an international setup. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.004]
- Your customers should be located in the country where your supplier stocks the products, so the supplier's location determines your target market. For example, US-based products are sold to US customers. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.005]
- For a store that targets a country where you do not live, use suppliers located in that country. Shipping and tariffs from abroad are the main reasons this matters. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0468.014]
- Dropshipping from a non-US country works if the supplier ships from a US warehouse or distribution center. The store can then be run under a US company set-up. Check the choice of entity with a forum or professional before setup. *(Older, 2019.)* [L0357.065]
- Expansion from North America into the EU is often impractical, because shipping can cost around ten times the North American rate. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.009]

### Running the business from abroad

- A seller based outside the US can run a store that sells to US customers without relocating, since the US market has many active sellers. Confirm the legal and tax requirements for your country and the US states you sell into before launch. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0961.092]
- A business owner living outside the US can still apply to US suppliers, provided the business is set up in the US. This usually means forming a US company from abroad. Confirm the business structure before applying, so the setup does not block supplier approval. [L0470.100]
- Running the store from a non-US company can still work with US suppliers, because suppliers receive payment in the same way. The legal side should be checked with an accountant or a community forum. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1085.082]
- Sellers from outside the US can follow the same setup process to sell into the US market. Only local market specifics differ by country. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1275.056]
- The business can be run from any country, as long as the operation is managed remotely. Many stores run from outside the US successfully. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1086.053]
- A store owner can target a country without living there. If a home country lacks enough high-ticket suppliers for a chosen niche, the business can target another market such as the US or UK. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0967.102]
- Selling into a larger market from a different country than your base is possible, so the home market is not a fixed limit. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0968.106]
- A dropshipping store can run semi-passively from abroad if customer contact, supplier contact and order handling are set up correctly from the start. It still needs regular check-ins, such as email checks several days a week, so it is not fully passive. [L0910.001]
- Calls, emails and orders arrive in the store's home time zone. A store owner working in a distant time zone should plan coverage for the hours they are asleep. The coverage can come from short routines or from delegated staff. [L0910.002]
- Slow travel, meaning staying at least one month in each location, reduces distractions and travel admin. It also keeps the daily routine stable enough to work efficiently. [L1441.002]

### Market testing and expansion order

#### Testing

- Validate a product in a large market such as the USA or Canada first. Then take the same proven product to another market, since the same product can perform very differently across countries. [L0549.012]
- Breaking ad results down by country showed the United States outperforming all other markets for a Facebook-driven store. The seller concentrated spend on the US for four to five months before testing other markets again. [L1127.009]
- Worldwide targeting combined with free shipping made profit difficult to manage. The worldwide spend was reduced and budgets moved to the top 5 to 10 countries. Scaling down a weak region can protect margin while the strongest markets grow. [L1127.010]
- Choose test and expansion markets by buying power, not only by cheap traffic. [L0361.052]
- Treat the first international order as a milestone that proves the model can scale. Selling the same product outside the home market shows that the business model can grow simply by adding volume and reach. [L1438.005]
- When a product works in one country, expand to other European markets such as Germany and France with a separate store for each market. Use a separate ad account or agency account to gather those markets' data, rather than mixing them in one store. [L1440.059]

#### Expansion within one store or separate stores

- One Shopify store can sell to Australia and Europe by adding those countries to the shipping options, so a separate store is not needed at the start. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0664.085]
- Build a separate country store, such as a.com.au domain, only when that market should carry a smaller set of brands, for example 20 of 100. The country store can keep the same logo and company information and list only the products chosen for that market. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0664.085]

#### Order of expansion

- Start with one country and scale within it before expanding abroad. The home market alone can supply enough revenue for most sellers. Countries with about 5 million (New Zealand) and 17 million (Netherlands) inhabitants were cited as workable markets in 2020. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.008]
- Test countries one at a time and keep the strongest market as the base. [L0694.033]

### Small home markets

- Sellers in a small home market such as New Zealand, Sweden or Sydney can still succeed. Check shipping costs and taxes to nearby markets first, and broaden your niche. Only expand to neighboring markets if shipping and duties are reasonable. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0468.015]
- Drop shipping can be started in a country outside the US, but the market and the number of buyers in that country affect the size of the business. A small local market can be a poor fit, so consider the US or other large markets. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0801.171]
- A small local market can still work when the store's niche is well chosen. The best choice is often to test a local market alongside the larger US market. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0801.172]
- In a small home market, widen the product range so you can find enough suppliers and products to sell. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0968.106]
- Small-population markets often lack enough search volume for a narrow niche. In that case, move up one level to a broader category that gets enough traffic. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.010]
- Start with one country and scale within it before expanding abroad. Countries with about 5 million (New Zealand) and 17 million (Netherlands) inhabitants were cited as workable markets in 2020. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.008]

### Business entity types

#### Sole proprietor

- A sole proprietorship makes the owner and the business legally the same. Personal and business assets are mixed and exposed to business liabilities. [L0695.001]
- A sole proprietor is a business run under the owner's personal name with no formal registration. An LLC is formed through formal documents with the state. An S-corp is a tax designation applied to an existing LLC. A corporation is generally not needed for a dropshipping business. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1415.002]
- You can legally start a US dropshipping business as a sole proprietor. It is wiser to form an LLC from the beginning, because later conversion to an LLC after selling a business adds work. Choose a generic business name, form the LLC in your state, and do not spend long deciding. Any structure works if you commit and move forward. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1415.001]
- A business can start as a sole proprietor with an EIN, and an LLC is not required at the beginning. Sole proprietor, LLC and S-corp each have trade-offs, so compare them before choosing. [L0970.010]
- Many beginners start as a sole proprietor, form an LLC and later an S-corp with an accountant once sales come in. A general business name works for several future stores. [L0934.005]
- Many founders start as a sole proprietor using a personal bank account. They later form a corporation with a business bank account and an accountant once profits arrive. A new business can also set up separate entities from day one if it wants full separation. [L0941.003]
- The legal entity choice is a personal decision. Many owners start as a sole proprietor because the risk is low, and then set up a corporation once the business is making money. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1068.014]
- An LLC is the recommended structure from the start. A sole proprietorship is only a reasonable shortcut for someone with almost nothing to lose who wants to avoid an extra filing. [L0695.002]

#### LLC

- An LLC is a separate business entity that shields personal assets from the business. [L0695.001]
- Outside the US, a dropshipping business usually has two legal structure options: a sole trader or a limited liability company. Names differ by country, but Commonwealth countries use fairly similar structures. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0702.002]
- The legal structure, whether sole trader or company, is not visible to customers. It affects liability and taxes, not the storefront, checkout or customer experience. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0702.030]
- Use a generic business entity name that is not tied to one niche. Related niches such as desks and chairs can share one entity, while an unrelated niche such as greenhouses should get its own new entity. [L0970.008]
- An LLC name does not have to appear on the website. Many stores show only the store name, and the legal entity can stay private. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0358.050]
- The LLC itself does not need a website. Websites are built only for sales stores. [L0695.004, L0934.008]
- A new business name should be the umbrella company that owns related stores. Use a different company name for an unrelated niche. [L0971.032]
- Each store should have its own generic-named company rather than a store name in the company name. [L1114.009]
- A business address that is not the owner's home address is recommended. Do not use a virtual address for Google Business Profile, since it is not required and could be a concern for Merchant Center. [L1115.027]
- Consider using a DBA when the LLC name differs from the store name, so the store is legally doing business under its public name. [L1115.029]
- A DBA can officially link a store URL to the business entity. The legal entity name is what goes on banking and tax forms and into the business information in Shopify settings, while the store name can differ. [L0970.009]

#### S-corp

- For a US business that is in it for the long term, an LLC elected to be taxed as an S-corporation is considered the best structure. Part of the income is paid as a set salary and the rest as distributions, which are taxed at a lower rate. This can save money. [L0970.005]
- The S-corp tax election can be made from the start of the business rather than only after it has grown. Whether it makes sense depends on the owner's situation, so a tax professional should confirm it. [L0970.006]
- An S-corp tax election becomes more advantageous as the business earns more profit, so it is typically considered after revenue grows, not at the start. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1415.003]

Sources differ on timing. Use L0970.006 when a tax professional confirms that an early election fits your situation. Use L1415.003 as the default, which is to consider the election after revenue grows.

#### Co-mingling

- Mixing personal and business funds (co-mingling) removes the liability protection of a business entity. Even when a personal account is used as a temporary workaround, keep the flows separated where possible. [L0970.007]

### Entity grouping for related and unrelated niches

#### Core rule

- For related niches (for example office chairs and desks), operate under a single LLC. For completely unrelated niches (for example office furniture and greenhouses), create separate business entities to compartmentalize finances and simplify future sale of individual stores. [L0251.039]
- Closely related businesses (for example modern office desks and modern office chairs) share one business entity, bank account, and credit card. Unrelated businesses receive their own business entity, bank account, and credit card. Determine relatedness by asking: Would the same customer buy from both? Would I cross-promote them? [L0253.008]
- Group related stores under one LLC and put unrelated niches into separate LLCs. This keeps money from being mixed, simplifies accounting, and makes it easier to sell related stores as a bundle later. [L0941.002]
- Each niche should stand as its own business line. Related niches that customers can cross-promote can sit under one parent company, while unrelated niches need a separate entity. This lets stores be sold together as a network. [L0667.075]

#### Related stores

- Stores in related niches are grouped under one LLC. This is the name used on supplier forms, the bank account and tax filings, while each store keeps its own brand and domain. [L0695.004]
- Related stores that are cross-promoted with each other can share one business entity and one EIN. [L0934.009]
- Example: a trampoline store and a bounce house store, which would be cross-promoted, share one corporation and one EIN. [L0934.010]
- Closely related stores sell better as a bundle or network, since buyers value a group of stores in one niche. Keep a separate legal entity for each group of related businesses so accounting stays clean and the group can be sold as one unit. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0468.013]
- Stores that belong to the same network of related niches should sit under one company. A future buyer of one store usually wants a non-compete covering the whole network. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.003]
- Sites in related niches that target the same customers can share one LLC. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0969.027]
- Stores that are indirectly related, for example through cross-promotion, should be kept under one business entity so they can be sold together later. [L1114.009]
- One company entity can own several stores with different store names and domains. The company's assets are the domains, the store design, the customer lists and the supplier relationships, so a single entity can hold multiple stores. [L1114.066]
- One company can own several store domains. Form one company, such as a US LLC, and build each store under it. Each store can run as its own brand under the same legal entity. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0469.002]
- One LLC can own several niche stores, but each store should keep its own identity and accounts. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0358.050]

#### Unrelated stores

- Unrelated business areas, such as a store in a different niche, get a separate LLC. This keeps the books and accounting clean and makes it simpler to sell one business later without affecting the others. [L0695.005]
- A store in an unrelated niche should get its own LLC and EIN, so its accounting can be separated if it is sold later. [L0934.009]
- Example: a horse saddle store that is unrelated to the trampoline and bounce house stores gets a new LLC and a new EIN. [L0934.010]
- A store in an unrelated niche should be under a separate LLC, so it can be sold or audited without affecting others. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0969.027]
- A new, unrelated niche should go under a separate entity, even if the same owner runs it with the same skills. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.003]

#### Account separation

Sources differ on how far separation goes inside a shared entity. Apply the one that matches the relationship:

- Closely related stores share one bank account and one credit card. [L0253.008]
- Each store keeps its own identity and accounts, even within one LLC. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0358.050]
- Each store keeps its own brand and domain under the same entity. [L0695.004]

### EINs and US registration

- A US EIN is recommended for doing business in the US. This applies to a Canadian business selling into the US. [L0360.084]
- A business can start as a sole proprietor with an EIN. [L0970.010]
- Related stores that are cross-promoted share one EIN. An unrelated store gets its own EIN. [L0934.009]

### Taxes and administration

- Setting up a company in a country with a favorable tax structure, such as Ireland, can be considered for selling to other markets. The tax benefit must be checked for the specific business before it is relied on. [L0359.007]
- Confirm the legal and tax requirements for your country and the US states you sell into before launch. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0961.092]
- Dropshipping from a non-US country can be set up under a US company. Check the choice of entity with a forum or professional before setup. *(Older, 2019.)* [L0357.065]
- Running the store from a non-US company can work with US suppliers. Check the legal side with an accountant or a community forum. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1085.082]
- To check whether a home-based business needs a local license after moving to a new state, search for that state's business registration or business license page on a.gov website and call the phone number listed there. Ask your question directly, since the office can give the state-specific answer. [L0970.011]
- Spending dozens of hours researching where to register a business or how to minimize tax before the business earns meaningful profit wastes time that costs more than the savings. Register it where you already live while income is small. [L1515.016]

### Common mistakes

- Launching a cross-border high-ticket model without pricing in freight, customs duties and currency conversion can leave little or no margin. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0781.004]
- Mixing personal and business funds removes the liability protection of a business entity. [L0970.007]
- Spending dozens of hours researching where to register a business or how to minimize tax before profits arrive wastes time. [L1515.016]
- Choosing a market by cheap traffic alone, instead of buying power, makes a poor test market. [L0361.052]
- Expanding abroad before the home market is proven. Start with one country and scale within it first. *(Older, 2020.)* [L1277.008]

## Niche and market selection strategy

### Niche versus general store

- Build niche-specific stores rather than one general store that mixes unrelated categories. Mixed general stores convert much worse and make it hard to stand out from competitors. This applies as of 2020 (older). [L1277.001]
- Combining unrelated categories is usually not worth it. [L1277.001]
- Sources differ on the stage. General stores are common for testing in the current landing page environment, and a single proven product can later move to its own store. Use a general store to test; move to a niche store once a product has proven itself. [L0950.013]
- Wayfair.com is an example of a general store that sells almost everything, with well over 90% of its products dropshipped. It grew into a billion-dollar, publicly traded company with hundreds of employees, while most dropshipping businesses build niche-specific stores instead. [L0766.003]
- Wayfair.com was previously known as CSN Stores and rebranded to Wayfair about three years before the lesson was recorded. Its general-store model differs from the niche-specific stores most dropshippers build. [L0766.024]
- A narrow niche builds authority with shoppers, which tends to raise conversion rates and make paid ads more profitable. A store that tries to sell almost everything loses that focus and the advantage that comes with it. [L0964.155]
- Single-niche stores that meet the niche criteria convert better and produce more profit per ad dollar than multi-niche stores. (2023) [L1283.064]
- Build a niche-specific store rather than combining unrelated niches, because focused stores convert better and make paid ads more profitable. (older: 2022) [L0665.001]
- Add cross-sell accessories only when they are used together with the core product. Products that people do not buy together belong on separate stores. (older: 2022) [L0665.001]
- Keep the store focused on one niche, with only products that belong to that niche. Add products from parallel niches only if the research numbers stop matching, which means niching up. [L1114.008]
- Choose one product type rather than one single product, and build the whole store around it. A focused niche lets the store establish credibility and authority in that category. [L1346.002]
- Do not build a store with only one product. A single-product store is not recommended. (older: 2020) [L1277.002]
- A store that sells random, unrelated products, with no niche and no long-term plan, is a burner store. It looks built for quick sales, is unlikely to grow into a real business, struggles to earn trust, and is hard to sell later. [L1346.001]
- Narrow a new business to one niche category at the start instead of keeping many product ideas open, because a single focus makes it much easier to gain traction and sustain growth. (2025-26) [L1016.001]
- Serve one identifiable group of customers defined by a shared interest rather than by a single product, so you always have several profitable products to sell and do not depend on finding the next winning item. (older: 2021-22) [L0458.008]
- Focusing on one winning product creates a turn-and-burn business that falls apart when the product's popularity fades. Defining the niche by customer interests makes marketing easier and keeps the business stable. (older: 2021-22) [L0458.009]
- Large e-commerce companies such as Amazon cannot match the detailed service a niche business gives one customer group. You do not need to beat Amazon's size to earn a good income, only a small share of its market. (older: 2021-22) [L0458.010]
- Most winning dropshipping products are not tied to a narrow niche but appeal to a broad audience, such as anyone who wants to look good or take care of their body. Narrow niches tend to cap volume, since a very deep niche may only reach around 50 to 100 orders per day. [L0780.001]
- Sources differ on breadth. Broad-appeal products suit the product-winner search, while niche-specific stores suit a lasting business. Use the broad product to find a winner and the niche store to build on it. [L0780.001, L0665.001]
- Plan the store for the long term from the start. A store focused on one product type is more valuable if you later want to sell it, and it is more likely to last for years. [L1346.003]

### Defining the audience first

- When a product idea looks too competitive, change the focus from the product to the customer: first define who buys, then decide which products they need. Niching down on products alone does not change what the site says to the buyer. (older: 2021-22) [L0905.002]
- Case: a general e-bike store struggled for a long time with broad positioning. Its owner narrowed the whole site, content and design to one buyer group, hunters who use e-bikes built for hunting.
