The store reached about $5 million per year in revenue, while most general e-bike stores in the same market earned little. (older: 2021-22) [L0905.004]
- A niche does not need to be a single-brand or single-category niche. If the products are sold on sites that also cover other niches, the niche is still fine, and a sub-niche can be split off and owned as its own niche. (2023) [L0835.005]
- Enthusiast or hobby niches are not a required filter. The highest-revenue businesses in this example were not hobby-driven, so niche choice should rest on other criteria such as search volume and supplier availability. (2023) [L0835.006]
- Shopping for a store's niche should first target products people already search for, which gives a store traffic and sales as soon as it launches. Once a store has traffic, an email list and social reach, new brands can be introduced to the store and promoted. [L1114.065]
- A market can be worth shortlisting even if you have no personal use for its products, when demand is clearly growing in your target country. Growth in a market is a valid reason to keep an idea in the running. (older: 2021-22) [L1329.023]
- Do not niche into the example products used as course illustrations, such as chandeliers and stand-up paddle boards. Many beginners pick them for that reason alone and the market is crowded. (2023) [L0964.002]
- For a low-interaction business that can be automated as far as possible, the upper middle class is the preferred target demographic. Lower interaction means less customer service load, which lets the owner avoid being tied up in support work and build a lifestyle business. [L1518.009]

### Goal and time horizon

- Decide your goal before searching for products: a quick short-term profit, a dropshipping business, or scaling a product you already have. The goal determines which kind of product to look for. [L0706.001]
- Choose products and niches for a horizon of three, five and ten years, not the next few months. Temporary events such as COVID-19 should not decide the niche on their own. [L0359.006]
- Choose evergreen niches whose products keep selling for years, so the store has no natural end date. You can sell the store later if you stop operating it. (2023) [L0934.087]

### Research sequence and first-niche pragmatism

- Niche research works as a fixed sequence in which each stage depends on the one before it. The brainstorm of at least 50 niche ideas must be finished before the validation tests are run: price points, margins, demand, seasonality, loyalty and competition. [L1057.008]
- A niche can be submitted for a verification review before any time is invested in it. Physical product niche ideas can be checked, and the review tells you whether to proceed, pivot or niche sideways. (2023) [L0835.004]
- The first niche does not need to be perfect. A viable but non-ideal niche will still generate profit and provide operational learning. While the first store runs profitably, build a second store with superior niche research. Continuous improvement and dual-store operation beats endless research with no launches. (older: 2020) [L0268.072]
- When a niche meets the criteria but is slow, work with what you have before switching: onboard every supplier you can and make the most of those brands. A store netting about $2,000 profit per month is worth keeping and building on rather than starting over. (older: 2020) [L1277.007]
- If a niche fails the criteria, add sub-niches. If it still fails, narrow to a higher-level niche. (2023) [L1283.064]
- Choose a niche where one customer can be sold several different products over time. Repeat sales should be built into the model from the start, not added later. (older: 2021-22) [L1485.016]
- Build the business on a viable high-ticket product market. The marketing system only works on a viable market; if the niche or products are weak, marketing alone will not fix the business, so product validation must come first. (older: 2021-22) [L0414.002]

### Recurring revenue and lifetime value

- Choose markets with recurring revenue potential, meaning customers buy from you again at least once after their first purchase. Recurring revenue is a core marketing concept for this model. (older: 2021-22) [L0994.018]
- Avoid markets where a customer buys only once every five to ten years, because you must keep acquiring new customers to grow. That approach is more expensive and yields lower profit, even though it can still work. (older: 2021-22) [L0994.020]
- A business that sells a single high-ticket product bought once every 5 to 10 years, or never again, must keep acquiring new customers and cannot market profitably to existing ones. Growth is capped by how many new buyers enter the market, so such a niche is a weaker choice than one with repeat purchases. (older: 2021-22) [L1485.002]

### Seasonality and evergreen choices

Seasonality checks and rules:

| Situation | Guidance | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Google Trends, 5-year view, minor fluctuations, stays above 50% of peak year-round (kegerator example) | Acceptable | [L0268.074] (older: 2020) |
| Search volume drops to near-zero in the off-season | Avoid | [L0268.074] (older: 2020) |
| First store | Avoid seasonal niches | [L0468.011, L0469.004, L1281.026] (older: 2020, 2021, 2022) |
| Existing seasonal store, low months | Use geo-targeted ads that reach only warmer states | [L0468.011] (older: 2020) |
| Later store, off-season demand strong | Consider a seasonal niche | [L0469.004] (older: 2021) |

- Use Google Trends' 5-year view of search volume to assess seasonality. Acceptable seasonality shows minor fluctuations without dropping significantly from peak; the kegerator example stays above 50% of peak year-round. Avoid a niche that drops to near-zero during the off-season. (older: 2020) [L0268.074]
- Avoid seasonal niches for a first store, because revenue falls for part of the year regardless of execution. For an existing seasonal store, use geo-targeted ads that reach only warmer states during the low months. (older: 2020) [L0468.011]
- Avoid seasonal niches for the first store, even when off-season demand is strong, because they add demand swings and many extra moving parts. Start with an evergreen niche and consider a seasonal niche for a later store. (older: 2021) [L0469.004]
- Avoid seasonal products for a first store. Seasonal businesses take longer to reach a level that can replace a job, while a year-round product can stabilize income faster. (older: 2022) [L1281.026]
- An evergreen brand can keep a stable core of products that sell all year and add event-based campaigns, such as Mother's Day, to capture busier periods. Event products are timed to the calendar, while evergreen products carry the business between events. [L0502.049]

### Competition and saturation

- Competition test: if more than 50% of the products on the first page of organic Google Shopping results come from 10 or more different stores, competition for that product is considered too high. (older: 2022) [L0969.004]
- A competitive niche is not suited to everyone. More competition usually means a bigger market, and returns can be higher, but it demands more time, attention, money and patience. [L0920.034]
- A competitive niche needs more time, attention, money and patience, and a bigger market usually means higher returns. It is a poor fit for a store you intend to flip or sell within one year. [L1357.045]
- Brand loyalty in a niche means only a few product types dominate, not a few stores selling many brands. A niche with many brands and few stores is acceptable. (2024) [L0856.117]
- Sources differ on competition. The test in [L0969.004] is a hard filter; a niche that passes it can still be competitive and demand more resources, as described in [L0920.034, L1357.045]. Apply the test first, then judge the resources you can commit.

### Mass desire and demand direction

- Advertising can only direct a desire that already exists among many people; it cannot create that desire. Treat the job as finding a shared, already-active desire and channelling it onto your product. [L0330.001]
- Do not run against a rising public demand. A campaign that pushes a product in the opposite direction of a strong trend fails even with a large budget, so follow the trend and pick the product angle that matches it. [L0330.003]
- Automotive examples show the rule:
  - In 1948 a manufacturer built a shorter, squatter car with more interior room while the public wanted longer, lower, wider cars; a multimillion-dollar campaign failed. [L0330.004]
  - In the mid-1950s the market demanded horsepower, and brands that sold power sold well. A major brand that spent heavily on safety messaging saw no results and the following year released its most powerful engines. [L0330.004]
  - A good, inexpensive compact car backed by heavy advertising also failed when demand shifted sharply to cheap, simple, economical compacts. [L0330.004]
- Mass desire is a private want shared publicly by enough people to repay the cost of advertising, manufacturing, selling and profit. A market is born when such a shared desire becomes large enough to be profitable, and it can range from a few thousand people to tens of millions. [L0330.005]
- Rate each candidate mass desire on three dimensions before choosing it: urgency and intensity (how strongly people need it), staying power (how often it repeats and never gets satisfied for good), and scope (how many people share it). Choose the desire that scores highest on all three together. [L0330.006]
- A product usually satisfies two to four mass desires, but an ad can carry only one dominant desire at a time. Choosing that desire is the most important decision in writing the ad, because the headline depends on it and no later copy can rescue a wrong choice. [L0330.007]
- Mass desires come from permanent forces, such as instincts (attractiveness, health, virility) and technological problems that remain unsolved, or from forces of change, such as trends, style shifts and mass education. Permanent forces require a fresh way to claim the same benefit. Changing forces require spotting the shift early and knowing when it becomes statistically significant. [L0330.008]
- Channel an existing mass desire onto your product in three steps:
  1. Choose the single most powerful desire that can be applied to the product, scored on urgency, staying power and scope. [L0330.009]
  2. Acknowledge that desire, reinforce it, or offer the means to satisfy it in one statement in the headline. Start the headline with the product if the prospect knows it, with the desire if the prospect knows only the desire, and with the problem if the prospect knows only the problem. [L0330.009]
  3. Show, step by step in the body copy, how each product performance inevitably satisfies that desire. [L0330.009]
- Supplements sell far more than gym services because a pill delivers the result with almost no time, effort or sacrifice. A rough claim puts the supplement market at about ten times the fitness industry, which is not verified. [L0549.014]

### Price, margin and shipping viability

- Use the average price from Google Shopping results, not the median, to understand what products sell for across a price range. The average gives a realistic view of the price points you will list. (older: 2021) [L0853.033]
- Niches with an average order value of about $300 to $500 are a practical option for a store with limited cash. Higher-ticket niches around $3,000 are also viable if you can cover the cost of one order. (older: 2021) [L0853.061]
- The number of products in a niche and the price range matter more than the brands on marketplaces. A wide price range inside a niche is normal; for example, couches sell from about $150 to tens of thousands. (older: 2020) [L0468.019]

Heavy and large products:

- Large and heavy products are viable if the price is high enough to cover shipping. For example, a futon ($300), a leather sofa ($1,500) and a sectional ($3,000) have similar shipping costs, so the cheapest one may leave no margin. (2024) [L0971.048]
- Large and heavy products only work when the selling price is high enough to cover freight, because shipping cost stays roughly the same whatever the item's price. A product selling for $200 leaves too little margin after wholesale cost, card fees, ads and free shipping, so cheap heavy items should not be sold or advertised. (2023) [L1114.001]
- Worked margin example (2023), [L1114.002]:

| Item | Selling price | Cost basis | Left for the rest |
|---|---|---|---|
| Item with wholesale cost about 50% of selling price | $200 | about $100 wholesale | about $100 for card fees, ads and shipping |
| Three-seater fabric sofa | $1,500 | $750 | $750 for shipping and ads |
| Custom Italian leather sofa | $15,000 | not stated | same shipping cost range as the $1,500 sofa |

- Within a heavy-goods niche, pick a sub-niche with enough price range to make money, such as futons at $200 versus premium sofas at $1,500 or more. Cheaper items in that niche should be kept off paid traffic and may be kept only for organic visitors. (2023) [L1114.003]
- When shipping is expensive, as in Australia where a small envelope costs about $2, start with products small enough to fit in an envelope. [L1437.001]
- Products with complex installation can be sold, but the operator must plan installation support, which adds work. Hands-off products have less friction. (2022) [L0969.095]

### Returns and return-rate risk

- Returns on a low-margin dropship product are often cheaper to absorb than to build a returns system for, if the return rate stays around 5%. Early on, accepting the loss and moving on can be the rational choice. Stricter rules can be added later for customers who abuse returns, such as buyers ordering several clothing items and sending some back. [L0552.001]
- Case: a motorcycle gear brand sold products at around $120 with goods costing $70 to $80, and returns were costly, so the margin was too thin. A higher-ticket product needs a consultative sale and enough margin to absorb returns. [L0552.038]
- Apparel has a return rate well above the e-commerce average, mainly because customers misjudge size or material. Apparel stores should plan for higher returns and watch the return rate closely. [L0838.006]
- Large apparel retailers can absorb very high return rates because they own their brands and have a large database of repeat buyers. A dropshipping store without these advantages cannot absorb the same return rate, so high-return categories are risky for it. [L0769.020]
- If you run a lifestyle dropshipping business with limited customer service time, avoid niches with high return rates. Returns require constant customer service work that does not fit a lean operation. [L0769.021]

### Supplier availability and niche pivots

- If your chosen niche has fewer suppliers than expected, pivot within the niche before starting over. Expand, for example, from office chairs to office chairs plus desks, or office chairs plus ergonomic accessories. Niching sideways often provides enough suppliers without abandoning your research. (2023) [L0251.053]
- Use the supplier check together with the verification review and the competition test: a niche with few suppliers is not dropped automatically but first widened sideways. [L0251.053, L0835.004, L0969.004]

### Branded categories and B2B or B2C fit

- Manufacturers of branded products spend hundreds of millions of dollars on marketing to create customer loyalty. This spending makes it impossible for dropship stores to compete in those categories, even if the manufacturer approves dropshipping. [L0319.002]
- Manufacturer approval does not guarantee dropshipping viability. Even if Apple approved iPad dropshipping, you could not profitably compete, because major retailers like Best Buy and the Apple Store already own customer traffic and loyalty for those products. [L0319.006]
- Solo e-commerce operators cannot profitably sell branded products, because they lack the hundreds of millions of dollars in annual marketing budgets that manufacturers like Sony and Bose deploy. Branded categories require either manufacturer distribution partnerships or equivalent marketing spend, both unavailable to individual operators. [L0319.019]
- A drop-shipping store is not directly comparable to a brand that makes its own products, such as a luxury label. The goal is to be the best-known store for a niche's buyers, not to match a manufacturer's scale. (older: 2021) [L0853.011]
- Renting products to end consumers online is a poor fit for a lifestyle business, because it creates repeated back-and-forth exchanges and support problems. Renting to businesses, such as party tents or inflatable bounce houses, is workable. (older: 2021) [L0801.185]

### Operator fit and passion

- You do not need to be passionate about the niche you sell. Focus passion on building and growing the business. Profitability and business success matter more than personal interest in products. (older: 2021) [L0270.041]
- The first store does not have to be the final answer; a profitable first store funds the learning for the second. [L0268.072]

## Customer, competitor and positioning research

### Customer avatars and target audience

- The target audience definition has two goals. First, understand not only what people do but why they do it and what they need. Second, communicate in a way they understand and connect with, so they take the action you want. A precisely defined audience makes the message more relevant, selects better products, and improves user experience, conversion rates and retention. [L0497.001]
- Trying to serve everybody dilutes the message. A broad audience makes marketing fail, so narrow the audience to a specific segment before writing any messaging. [L0497.002]
- (Older: 2021-22) Customer avatar work is a foundational marketing task. If you already have current profiles, review them only for accuracy. If you have never done it, complete the full sheet. [L0907.004]
- (Older: 2021-22) There is no fixed number of customer avatars that applies to every business. The right count depends on the market, the niche and the product category, so decide it separately for each one. [L0907.031]
- Answer these four questions in order before building any funnel: [L0523.001]
  1. Who is my dream client: the specific person I most want to work with, including their traits, goals, passions and income level?
  2. Where do they spend time online: which sites, groups, newsletters, blogs and platforms do they use?
  3. What bait will attract them and repel everyone else?
  4. What result do I want to deliver to them? A business sells results, not products.
- Market and design a product where the customer journey is understood. Sell a product for the real reason people buy it, such as the feeling of control or the desire for a hobby. [L1426.039]

### Customer awareness pyramid

**Structure and stages**

- (Older: 2021-22) The customer awareness pyramid shows the five stages a buyer passes through before purchasing: completely unaware, problem aware, solution aware, product aware, and ninja aware. It represents the whole market for a product. Each higher stage contains fewer people, so the bottom stage is the largest and the top stage is the smallest. [L0481.001]
- (Older: 2021-22) Customer awareness follows a five-stage pyramid: completely unaware, problem aware, solution aware, product aware and ninja aware. Each buyer passes through these stages before purchasing, usually without conscious awareness of the steps. [L1117.006]
- (Older: 2021-22) The five stages in practice: [L1117.007]
  - **Completely unaware:** the person does not know they have a problem. They experience the problem a product solves without knowing it, and they form the largest part of the potential market. [L0481.003, L1117.007]
  - **Problem aware:** the person knows the problem but not the solution. They begin searching for a fix. The segment is still large but smaller than the completely unaware stage. [L0481.003, L1117.007]
  - **Solution aware:** the person knows which type of solution fits but has not chosen a product type. [L0481.004, L1117.007]
  - **Product aware:** the person has narrowed down to a product type and compares variants. [L0481.004, L1117.007]
  - **Ninja aware:** the person knows the exact product they want and only decides where to buy it. [L0481.004, L1117.007]
- (Older: 2021-22) Ninja aware buyers make up roughly 10% or less of the total market at any given time. Each stage is a smaller slice of the pyramid than the one below it. [L0481.005]
- (Older: 2021-22) If the best product is sold by only one seller, the ninja aware buyer has no choice of retailer. If several sellers offer it, as with reselling, the buyer still chooses a retailer at the final stage. [L0481.006]

**Problem first**

- (Older: 2021-22) E-commerce sells solutions to problems or desires, not products alone. The buyer first has a problem, then turns it into a want or need, and only then looks for the product. Marketing must therefore start from the problem the product solves. [L0481.002]
- (Older: 2021-22) Example of a beginner surfer: first the person wants to surf, then realises a surfboard is required (problem aware). Next they weigh buying against renting and decide to buy because they plan to surf for years (solution aware). They then compare board types for beginners, choose one based on budget and reviews (product aware), and finally pick a seller and pay (ninja aware). [L1117.008]

**Passing through the stages**

- (Older: 2021-22) Every buyer passes through all awareness stages, mostly subconsciously, as a natural process of education before buying. Ignoring this process lowers the conversion rate. [L0481.007]
- (Older: 2021-22) The time a customer takes from first noticing a need to buying varies widely, from a few days to several months. Low-cost, simple products usually have short journeys. Expensive or complex products, such as a $3,000 kitchen appliance, usually need longer consideration. [L1117.017]

**Mapping the pyramid to the funnel**

- (Older: 2021-22) The traditional sales funnel corresponds to the awareness pyramid turned upside down. Top of funnel means completely unaware or problem aware. Middle of funnel means solution aware or product aware. Bottom of funnel means ninja aware, ready to buy. [L0481.008]
- (Older: 2021-22) The awareness pyramid is an inverted sales funnel. Completely unaware is the top of the funnel, solution aware sits mostly in the middle, and product aware and ninja aware form the bottom, where buyers are closest to purchasing. [L1117.009]
- The two sources above place product aware buyers differently. L0481.008 puts them in the middle of the funnel, and L1117.009 puts them at the bottom with ninja aware. Use the middle placement for a three-part top, middle and bottom view. Use the bottom placement when the question is which buyers are closest to purchasing. [L0481.008, L1117.009]
- (Older: 2021-22) The awareness pyramid is a framework for the customer journey that also applies beyond copywriting. It can guide email marketing, retargeting and the type of content published for the business. [L0904.018]

**Where growth comes from**

- (Older: 2021-22) Many e-commerce businesses focus only on the bottom of the funnel (product aware and ninja aware). That segment is small at any time and cannot grow. The business stops scaling once it has saturated the available traffic there. [L1117.010]
- (Older: 2021-22) The market is far larger at the completely unaware, problem aware and solution aware stages than at the product aware and ninja aware stages. Scaling therefore requires reaching people earlier in the journey. [L1117.011]
- (Older: 2021-22) Be present at every stage of the pyramid, not only at the bottom of the funnel, where competitors already hold the buyer's attention. [L0414.004]

**Order of work**

- (Older: 2021-22) Before building traffic, work through the Know component first. Understand the ideal customer, the competitors, the financial numbers and the business goals. Then move on to Convert, Educate, Promote, Develop and Consolidate, in that order. [L0414.007]

### Market segmentation and niches

- Market segmentation groups an audience into segments that share common needs and characteristics and react similarly to a given marketing message, so each segment can receive a tailored message. [L0497.003]
- Market segmentation means grouping people who share characteristics so you can serve them with a message fitted to them. In a crowded market, narrowing the audience can shrink competition, so a product already on the market can still win by targeting a new segment. [L0999.001]
- Segmenting the audience is a way to find a less competitive position, even for a product that already exists elsewhere. Testing one specific audience can be worth doing before a broad approach when platforms push toward broad targeting. [L0999.002]
- Any product can sell when the audience is narrowly defined and the ad copy, creative, landing page and emails all address the same pain point. Choosing a specific audience and angle matters more than the product itself. [L1194.001]
- About 10% of a broad audience may recognise dropshipping. A large audience of 100 million therefore still leaves about 90 million potential buyers. Focus on service quality and product quality to win these buyers. [L1213.076]
- A market niche is a group of prospects with well-defined, shared requirements, such as delivery to a job site, measured quantities or delivery within hours. Find niches by listing the specific needs of a group and checking whether your strengths meet them. Use your previous industry experience to spot niches others have missed. [L0770.011]

### Identifying competitors

- If you resell other brands, your closest competitors sell the exact same brands and products. Second-level competitors sell the same type of product, even under other brands, and should still be listed. [L0893.008]
- If you sell your own product, competitors are those selling a similar competing product, including popular look-alike products sold only on Amazon. Resellers should leave out Amazon, eBay and similar marketplaces and focus on standalone websites that appear in the same search results as you. [L0893.009]
- (Older: 2021) A competitor is a store that sells for several brands without its own manufacturing and without a physical location (no warehouse, retail store or showroom). Two to three such online-only competitors with over 20 brands each is enough to call a niche drop-ship friendly. They do not all have to be in the exact niche. [L0834.003]
- (Older: 2021) Competitor sites that describe their products as factory direct are most likely drop-shipping from warehouses. Treat that wording as a strong sign of drop-shipping. [L0469.006]
- Large retailers such as Wayfair mostly drop ship the products they sell and also private label many of them, while holding little stock themselves. Treat such a retailer as a future competitor, not as a company to sell to or a model to copy directly. [L1224.038]

### Competitor analysis sheet

- A competitor analysis sheet uses one row per competitor, with these columns in this order. [L0893.007]

| Competitor Name | Website URL(s) | Same or Similar Products? | Traffic Sources | Email Marketing? | USP | Proof Points | Bonuses/Premiums | Risk Reversal | Price & Terms | Hook | Primary Marketing Promise | Features/Deliverables | Do they have content? | Comments |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| (one row per competitor) | | | | | | | | | | | | | | |

- (Older: 2021-22) The competitor analysis sheet is a living document. Review it at least once a year, ideally every six months, and whenever a new competitor appears or something new is learned. [L0893.005]
- (Older: 2021-22) The competitor sheet can be shared with a marketing team or employees so they can quickly see what competitors are doing. Keeping the sheet current is cheap and useful for the whole team. [L0893.006]
- Competitor analysis is an ongoing task, not a one-time check. Review the top three competitors regularly to see what they add, change or drop. [L0894.002]
- Store-level competitor analysis: compare the store's product page with the top two to three competitor stores found by searching the product in Google. Look at what the customer would see as a reason to buy elsewhere. [L0856.118]
- Shoppers often compare your store with other sellers of the same products. The purpose of a competitor review is to find what the top competitors skip or do poorly. Make those gaps the focus of your own store so buyers have a clear reason to pick you. [L0894.001]
- (Older: 2021-22) Once traffic sources are mapped across all competitors, channels that no competitor uses can reveal an open opportunity. Record gaps in the market as carefully as the channels competitors already use. [L0893.013]
- (Older: 2021-22) If a competitor's unique selling proposition or email marketing cannot be found, customers probably cannot find it either. Treat it as effectively absent from the market. Use this to judge which gaps in competitor offerings could be an opportunity. [L0441.011]

### Competing against large players

- Entering a market with a big competitor selling the same product carries risk, because the competitor already has trust, resources and scale. Look for differences in the country, the marketing angle or the platform that the competitor does not use. For example, check traffic in a specific country with SimilarWeb. [L1382.054]
- When a big brand already sells the same product, do not compete head-on with the same market and angle. Look for an unused country (check traffic via Similarweb), a different marketing angle, or a platform the brand does not use, such as Pinterest. [L1436.076]
- (Older: 2021) A niche-specialist store can compete with large generalists such as Wayfair, which sells over a million products. Build the store for one specific buyer type, study competitor weaknesses, and position yourself as the authority in that niche. [L0469.001]
- Choose a product category where the market has known problems, such as trust or quality issues, and solve them publicly. Being open about formulas, sourcing and testing is a differentiator in such categories, because most competitors avoid these topics. [L0334.011]
- (Older: 2020) Competing with Amazon works through specialist expertise, niche focus, buying guides, product bundles, free bonus gifts and direct relationships with brands. Amazon sells everything but offers less specific advice, fewer special bundles and less industry-specific content. [L0358.052]
- Do not try to beat Amazon on price. Its algorithms track the lowest price and drop their own price further. Once the price falls, Amazon rarely raises it again. [L1293.020]
- (Older: 2018) Before entering a niche, check whether the top competitors run strong sites with guides, videos and reviews. If they do, plan content of the same quality or better from day one instead of launching a basic store. [L1274.001]
- (Older: 2018) A store that copies the default supplier descriptions and a few basic images will rarely beat a competitor with richer content. Google will not rank the bare minimum above established high-quality sites. [L1274.002]

### Value, USP and differentiation

- (Older: 2021-22) Many conversion problems come from a weak understanding of the market rather than from the store's design. A business must know where it fits in its market and why customers should pick it over rivals before most marketing decisions get easier. [L0893.001]
- (Older: 2021-22) A business is fundamentally about solving real problems for other people, and products are the means of doing that. Growing a large e-commerce business requires communicating clearly how the business and its products solve problems that real people actually experience. [L0872.001]
- (Older: 2021-22) Answers about the market and the competition feed copywriting, on-site conversion work, ad copy, email content and retargeting ads. A clear picture of competitors makes all of these tasks easier. [L0893.003]
- (Older: 2021-22) Before choosing marketing actions, a store owner should be able to explain in a few lines why customers would buy from them rather than from competitors and what makes the business distinct. Understand the ideal customer first, then the competitors, and only then the point of difference. [L0893.037]
- (Older: 2021-22) The competitor analysis is a starting point for every later decision. Turn its findings into action items such as a new USP, a new email sequence or a new content plan. The purpose is to find where your business can differ from the others. [L0893.036]
- Define your business differentiator before scaling: what sets you apart from competitors, including your products and the business mission. This determines where you fit in the market, so it is the most important input for later planning. [L1268.001]
- Frame the business around the result you give customers rather than the product you sell. When the result is clear and valuable enough, price stops being the main barrier to buying. Identify the highest-level result you could deliver, even if most customers first buy smaller pieces. [L0523.005]
- Alex Homosi's value equation:

  ```
  value = (increase perceived likelihood of achieving the dream outcome, or getting away from pain) combined with (reduce time, effort and sacrifice)
  ```

  Dream outcome and perceived success probability go up. Time, effort and sacrifice go down, because those are scarce resources people try to conserve. [L0497.014]

### Price, quality and service strengths

- Every business has three areas of market strength: price, quality and service. In a competitive market a company generally cannot be the best in all three at once, so decide which combination you truly offer before choosing prospects. [L0770.008]
- Match your strengths to the segments that value those strengths most, and avoid segments whose priorities you cannot meet. A good offer aimed at buyers who rank a different factor first still wastes effort. [L0770.009]
- Classify a feature such as on-time delivery by what the business sells. For a packaging supplier it is part of service. For an overnight carrier, on-time delivery is the product's quality, and service covers tracking, billing and courtesy. The category decides which buyers will value it. [L0770.010]

### Qualifying prospects

- Sort every prospect into one of four groups. Spend most of your time only on the first group. [L0770.006]
  1. Needs, wants and can afford the product: ready to buy.
  2. Needs and can afford it but does not want it.
  3. Needs and wants it but cannot afford it.
  4. Needs, wants and can afford it but prefers another supplier.
- Because time and resources are limited, every call to a low-probability prospect costs the chance to reach a likely buyer. Qualifying out most of the market early is the normal way to work, not a missed sale. [L0770.007]
- Select customers who purchase online independently, accept standard delivery timelines without requesting constant updates, and are satisfied with the completed transaction. Avoid haggling customers, chronic complainers and those seeking continuous contact, as they demand excessive personal attention that conflicts with a self-managing business operation. [L0019.001]

### From segment to message

- Before touching any ad platform, understand your market, your audience and your customers. Ads run without that groundwork waste money and time on any platform, including Facebook, Google, YouTube, Amazon and Pinterest. [L0643.001]
- Paid ads should not show a buy-now offer to everyone. Most people in a market are not ready to purchase today. The rough benchmark is that under 10% of searchers are ready to buy now. Segment the audience by readiness and treat each segment with a different message. [L1386.001]
- The sales funnel runs Awareness, Interest, Learn, Shop, Buy, and each stage needs its own kind of message. First make people aware of the solution, then give them interest, then let them learn and compare, then let them shop and finally buy. Digital marketing and sales funnel models vary, but this five-stage sequence is the common baseline. [L1386.002]
- Process for each client, packaged as a repeatable product so it can be rolled out for every client and scaled: [L0643.044]
  1. Know the market.
  2. Choose a segment.
  3. Craft the message.
  4. Prepare the creative.
  5. Prepare the structure, which means the store or the product-specific landing page.

## Starting capital, margins and profit planning

### Startup budgets

**Older figures (2019 to 2022) are shown with their year. Check the date and the amount of outsourcing before using one.**

| Setup | Starting amount | Year | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Owner does all the work and outsources nothing | A few hundred USD, with the free 100 USD Google Ads credit usable as part of the first ad budget (older, 2019) | 2019 | [L0356.008] |
| Complete dropshipping business: domain (12 USD), first month of Shopify (29 USD), LLC formation (about 100 USD) (older) | 200 to 300 USD | 2021 | [L0270.036] |
| Launch-ready store: domain 5 to 20 USD, first 14 days of Shopify free, logo 20 to 30 USD from a freelance platform, then Shopify at 29 USD per month (older) | About 100 USD | 2021 to 2022 | [L0771.072] |
| High-ticket dropshipping in the 2021 setting, compared with thousands for Amazon FBA or a direct-to-customer brand with an initial stock batch (older) | Under 500 USD in total | 2021 to 2022 | [L0771.007] |
| Start-up investment for the business (older) | Under 1,000 USD, with profit possible within a few months | 2021 to 2022 | [L1481.008] |
| Starting a store yourself: Shopify at 29 USD per month, free 14-day trial, outsourced high-end web design about 2,000 USD (older) | Varies with outsourcing | 2019 | [L1002.004] |
| Starting a store yourself, doing the work and spending little | Under 100 USD | Undated | [L0941.005] |
| Initial store setup before the first sale | Roughly 100 to 200 USD | 2023 | [L0970.012] |
| No storefront or retail cost, so starting costs stay low | No large minimum order quantity unless running a private label concept | 2026 | [L1195.053] |
| First private label order | Typically 1,500 to 2,000 USD on average. Some sellers invested as little as 500 USD, others up to 20,000 USD | Undated | [L1153.005] |

- Store setup and launch are cheap. The higher figures come from outsourcing and from private label stock, not from the store itself. [L0941.005, L1002.004, L1153.005]
- Older 2019 to 2022 figures are not wrong, but Shopify and freelance prices have changed since then. Use them as a floor, not a budget. [L0356.008, L0270.036, L0771.072, L1481.008]

### Margin targets

**The sources give different margin figures. Each applies to a different measure and situation.**

| Measure | Value | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Typical gross margin for drop-shipped retail products (older, 2021 to 2022) | 20 to 30% | Standard drop-shipping in most niches | [L0511.002] |
| Gross margin in niches with unusually low competition (older, 2021 to 2022) | 50 to 60% | Niches with unusually low competition | [L0511.002] |
| Planning average for dropshipping gross margin | About 30% of the sale price | Starting planning figure. Each product still needs its own check against real supplier costs | [L1147.003] |
| Target gross margin for a store with free shipping | About 30%. Results of 25% or somewhat higher also occur | Store average, aiming near 30% | [L1179.002] |
| Target net profit for a new dropshipping store (older, 2022). The source labels this a gross margin target, but defines it after cost of goods, payment fees, shipping and advertising | About 30% | New store. Below this, optimize rather than panic | [L0969.001] |
| Minimum average net profit for a niche | 25% | Niche selection (the profit you keep after costs) | [L1081.005] |
| Typical profit for a store that relies only on paid ads | 20 to 25% | Paid-ads-only stores | [L1215.026] |
| Margin target per store running paid ads | 15 to 25% | Tracked continuously, since the figure changes daily and monthly | [L1431.003] |
| Net profit of standard drop-shipping operators, after shipping, card processing fees and capital expenditure (older, 2021 to 2022) | Often under 10% | Standard drop-shipping, which is why brands move toward products they control | [L0511.021] |
| Gross margin for private label, where the brand owner keeps the margin the retailer would otherwise take (older, 2021 to 2022) | 75 to 80% | Private label | [L0511.006] |

- The 20 to 30% gross figure and the 30% store target are not in conflict. The first describes the typical market, the second describes what a new store should aim for. [L0511.002, L1179.002]
- The 30% goal is an average across the whole store, not a rule that every product must meet. [L1179.003]
- Calculate margin before running ads. Aim for at least 3x margin on the product price to scale profitably, because thin margins are a common reason for sales without profit. [L1260.056]
- Margin depends on where the product sits in the supply chain (older, 2021 to 2022):
  - Keystoning: a retailer buys from a supplier at half the retail price, which gives the retailer a 50% margin. Retailers following manufacturer map pricing often work this way. [L0511.004]
  - Example: a retailer sells a 1,000 USD product bought from the manufacturer for 700 USD, keeping about 300 USD (around 30% margin) after its own costs of about 350 USD to make the product. With private labeling, the same 1,000 USD retail price against 350 USD cost gives a 65% margin. [L0511.005]
  - Private label markup: roughly 4x to 5x the landed product cost. For example, a product costing 10 USD should sell for 40 to 50 USD. [L0511.003]

### Gross margin versus net

- Dropshipping margins are thinner than in most other e-commerce models, so every sale has to be run deliberately to become profit. Revenue growth alone does not show whether the business makes money. [L0414.047]
- The first task is finding products that can be sold above their full landed cost. Every product and supplier should be checked against this before it is listed. [L1148.001]
- Free shipping is the common standard in dropshipping. The customer sees free shipping at checkout, and the shipping cost is paid out of the merchant's profit margin. [L1179.001]
- Set the planning figure as net profit, not revenue. [L1147.006]
- Revenue targets mean little without profit. A store should be proven profitable before it is scaled up. A store making 10,000 USD per month at a loss is not a success. [L1487.004]
- Growth goals should be set in profit and cash flow terms, so each decision is judged by what it does to money kept. [L1458.012]
- A product whose cost of goods is too high to be profitable at a realistic price should be dropped rather than forced. Spend the time on products with better margins. [L0640.036]

### Profit per order and break-even

**Margin per order determines how far ad spend can go.**

- Higher margins allow more advertising spend before break-even. With dropshipping, as paid advertising costs rise over time, low margins make the business unsustainable. Owning the brand and pricing accordingly keeps unit economics healthy longer. [L0227.003]
- Higher margins free cash for acquiring new customers. When customer lifetime value is the goal, profit per order can matter more than order value. [L0511.007]
- A business that earns more profit per sale can afford to spend more on ads and keep them running longer, which gives it an advantage over competitors with less margin per order. [L0740.015]
- A store with only paid ads needs upsells, cross-sells, customer win-backs and a focus on customer lifetime value to become profitable. [L1215.026]
- Use paid traffic only on products with real margin. Products with thin margins, such as those with heavy discounts, should rely on organic traffic. [L0551.053]
- Use example prices and costs to learn how the expense structure works, but do not copy them. Run your own product price, supplier cost, shipping fee and ad budget through the same profit calculation before launch. [L0759.035]

**Worked margin check for large items (older, 2021)**

1. Wholesale at 50% of MAP: a 1,500 USD sofa costs 750 USD, a 500 USD sofa costs 250 USD. [L0834.002]
2. Subtract shipping (here 150 USD). [L0834.002]
3. Reserve ad spend at a minimum return of 10x ROAS: 50 USD for the cheap sofa, 150 USD for the expensive one. [L0834.002]
4. Subtract the merchant fee (2.9% + 0.30 USD). [L0834.002]
5. Result as stated in the source: the cheap item is left with about 50 USD profit and the expensive one is strongly profitable. The same shipping cost can make one product break even and the other pay. [L0834.002]

**Store results (older, Source B 2021 to 2022)**

| Store | Period | Gross or total sales | Returns | Net sales | Orders | Sessions | Net profit | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hardtop metal gazebo, site about 2 to 2.5 years old | June | 806,805.49 USD gross | -68,596.17 USD | 738,209.32 USD | 402 | 80,733 (about 0.5% conversion) | 14%, 103,201.65 USD after all costs. The lesson called this typical for this site each month | [L0458.044] |
| Hardtop gazebo | One day, July | 23,275.20 USD total | -100.00 USD | 23,175.20 USD (from 9 orders) | 9 | 716 sessions, 649 visitors | 18%, 4,171.50 USD for the day | [L0458.045] |
| Infrared sauna | July | 251,671.35 USD gross | -23,721.80 USD | 227,949.55 USD | 134 | 23,382 (about 0.6% conversion) | 22%, 50,148.90 USD after all costs. Presented as one month's profit from one website | [L0458.046] |
| Air purifier | July | 69,607.60 USD gross | -4,437.60 USD | 65,170.00 USD | 137 | 24,288 | 19%, 12,336.68 USD for the month. Described as a couple of hours of work per day | [L0458.047] |

### Ad budget limits and stop-losses

**Limits on ad spend, by product and by day.**

- Maximum ad spend to acquire a customer is up to 10% of the product's MAP price. For example, a 1,000 USD product allows up to 100 USD per customer acquisition. [L0251.013]
- Set the target for every product at a return on ad spend of at least 10x, meaning each dollar of ads brings back at least 10 USD in revenue. Use this ROAS target as the main gate for whether a product keeps getting budget. [L1115.001]
- A business with a target of 10 or more ROAS is considered profitable at the Google Shopping level, but profit per sale must still clear the dollar threshold set for the business. [L0856.114]
- Start with a small daily ad spend, roughly 30 to 50 USD per day. Keep what produces results, cut what does not, and grow from there. [L0970.015]
- Scale gradually instead of jumping. For example, raise a 1,000 USD monthly budget to 1,500 USD and check how return on ad spend changes. Scaling means balancing how much you can spend while return on ad spend stays strong. [L0970.016]
- Ad spend is fully controllable. When ads stop converting well, lower the budget and optimize campaigns for profit at lower spend. [L0966.008]
- Knowing the return per ad dollar gives the confidence to raise the budget step by step. Example: 30 USD back per dollar spent in the last month. A growth limit for many owners is reluctance to spend more on traffic. [L0359.003]

**Stop-loss procedure when a store is not profitable and money is tight (older, 2020)**

1. Keep the Shopify subscription (about 29 USD per month) so the store stays open. [L0359.001]
2. Cancel every paid app that does not lead to sales. [L0359.001]
3. Stop paid traffic that does not convert. [L0359.001]
4. Cut all other unnecessary expenses. [L0359.001]
5. Consider a short contract or job with a small digital marketing business (SEO firm, pay-per-click agency or local marketing company) to earn income and keep skills and motivation up. [L0359.001]

- Closing a store during a pause is a mistake. The store keeps its setup, and the fixed cost is small compared with rebuilding it. Stop only costs that do not produce sales. [L0359.002]
- Monthly profitability should be consistent. If a store has some profitable months, find what separates good months from bad ones, such as ad return or product mix, rather than abandoning the niche. [L0664.050] (older, 2020)

**Review before shutting down a niche with unprofitable months (older, 2020)**

1. Compare the figures for good months and bad months. [L0664.051]
2. Check whether the difference comes from return on ad spend or another cause. [L0664.051]
3. Have someone review the store and its numbers to find the fix. [L0664.051]

**Gate summary**

| Gate | Threshold | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Ad spend per customer | Up to 10% of MAP | [L0251.013] |
| Return on ad spend per product | At least 10x | [L1115.001] |
| Profit per sale | Must clear the dollar threshold set for the business, even at 10+ ROAS | [L0856.114] |
| Margin before ads | At least 3x on product price | [L1260.056] |
| Paid traffic | Only on products with real margin | [L0551.053] |

### Cost structure

- Apart from traffic, a dropship store has very low fixed expenses. Ad spend is fully controllable. [L0966.008]
- Costs outside a brand's control: rising acquisition costs, shipping costs, software costs, and new tariffs on goods made outside the US. [L0955.001]
- Costs a brand can control: returns handling, support time, lost parcels, refunds and fraud. Reducing them protects margin. [L0955.001]
- Customer acquisition is usually one of the largest expenses, whether paid through Google, Bing, Facebook, Snapchat or TikTok ads or invested in SEO content. Ad prices tend to rise over time, so a brand must acquire customers more cheaply than competitors. (older, 2021 to 2022) [L0511.008]
- Standard drop-shipping operators often keep under 10% net profit once shipping, card processing fees and capital expenditure are paid, so a 20 to 30% gross margin leaves little room for error. (older, 2021 to 2022) [L0511.021]
- Review all paid software and cloud services. Switching from monthly to annual billing is a saving in itself, where cash flow allows. [L0575.010]

### Cash reserves and cash flow

- Sales cash arrives within days, so a dropshipping business does not need a credit card to carry a balance for months. The card works best as a payment tool with the balance cleared each cycle, not as financing. [L0699.008]
- Do not go into negative cash flow or debt to pay for growth projects such as content marketing. Invest in these only as the business grows. [L0970.002]
- Keep the business as the first priority. Do not inject money you may need for living costs or the emergency fund. [L1126.032]
- Reserve target from the investor timeline: the savings target was 30,000 USD, but the real need was six months of expenses, under 6,000 USD. [L1126.033]
- Pre-sales let a new business start with minimal cash flow. Cash can be conserved during product refinement and manufacturing by collecting money before production is committed. [L1016.006]

### Funding and capital

- Launch and ad spend should not be financed by a business loan or grant. Borrowed money tends to lead to overspending. Start lean with small ad budgets and increase spending only as cash flow allows. [L0966.009]
- A founder raised up to about 200K USD specifically to fund aggressive scaling, but only after knowing the store's margin and cash position. Outside funding should follow proven numbers. [L1213.027]
- Putting new money into the business is worthwhile when it can plausibly return about 10 times its cost. Dropshipping needs little startup capital. [L1126.032]
- Card rewards (older, 2021 to 2022):
  - Rewards on expenses you already plan to pay work as cost-free arbitrage. The spending happens anyway, and the card issuer shares part of its fee with you. [L0903.003]
  - Early-stage owners with high personal debt and poor credit often lose access to additional cards, so card-based reward programmes are easier to access before credit deteriorates. [L0903.027]
  - Classic arbitrage example: buy an item for 50 USD at a thrift store or auction and sell it for 500 USD to a buyer who values it. Card rewards on spending you would make anyway add a similar return with no extra cost. [L0903.040]

### Reinvestment and profit distribution

- First-time profit distributions do not need to wait a set period. After weeks of sales generating net profit, you can take a distribution (for example 3,000 USD from 5,000 USD net profit) and reinvest the rest into the business. [L0268.063]
- Reinvestment timeline from the lesson:
  - 2013: the presenter started with about 1,000 USD and launched a dropshipping store. [L1126.033]
  - 2014: the store produced about 3,000 to 7,000 USD per month in profit. [L1126.033]
  - 2016: a store built alone was sold for about 60,000 USD. A second store was sold in 2017, and a later store sold for 44,000 USD through the broker Empire Flippers. [L1126.033]
  - All proceeds were invested, starting with about 3,000 USD per month from profits. Within three years the index fund holdings reached over 200,000 USD, with about 37,800 USD of that from growth alone. [L1126.033]

### Tax-efficient growth spending

- Spending part of the profit on growth before taxes reduces taxable profit. Spending qualifies when it covers ads, new ad platforms, team members, contractors or coaching. It only makes sense when the spending is expected to bring more profit next year and the owner's lifestyle goals and salary are still covered. [L1344.006]
- Example: out of a 200,000 USD profit, 50,000 USD can be reinvested in ads, testing new ad platforms, hiring or contractors, and coaching. If that spending is expected to lift profit in the following years, it can be worth it. [L1344.007]

### Profit goals and income floor

- Target monthly profit (net profit, not revenue) is the starting planning figure for a first store. Derive the required unit count backwards from it. In the lesson's example, the planning target was 3,000 USD net profit per month. [L1147.006]
- Keep the day job until the new business produces enough cash flow to cover your own costs. Quitting early removes the income that lets you absorb early mistakes and keep working toward the goal. [L1438.002]
- Fund the brand from part of a regular salary. Cover living costs first, then invest the remainder in inventory, start-up costs and monthly growth. The founder put about 60% of his salary into the brand. This works best with few fixed commitments such as rent or dependants. [L1438.001]
- A store that is not yet profitable should first keep its Shopify subscription and cut costs that do not produce sales, while looking for income to protect the owner's finances. [L0359.001]

#### Further details

- For this model, a startup budget of a couple of hundred dollars was described as enough to begin. [L0247.004]

## Launch plan and timeline

### Step sequence from niche to live store

**Order of decisions**

- Settle the niche before building the store, because product selection, supplier sourcing and store design all depend on it. [L1080.010]
- Choose the market, the product types and a rough picture of the target customer before building anything. The website and the supplier outreach both depend on these choices. [L1060.001]
- A weak market or product choice affects every later stage: the website, supplier contact and supplier approval. [L1059.002]
- Run the path from joining to a live store as one ordered sequence, completing each step before the next. Do not park steps under "come back to this later" when they are required for launch. [L0555.003]
- Advanced growth tactics, such as negotiating supplier exclusivity, referral and influencer marketing, advanced SEO, advanced email marketing and additional paid ad platforms, are not day-one tasks. Defer them until the basic store is live and has made its first sales. [L0555.001]
- Studying a long list of advanced topics before the first store is live overwhelms beginners and delays the first sale. Complete the basic setup in sequence first. [L0555.002]

**Build sequences by source**

Source A (undated), dropshipping store build sequence:

1. Choose a niche and product type that suits dropshipping and has profit potential. [L0506.001]
2. Validate the niche ideas with real market numbers that can be gathered free online, cross off weak ideas and favor the niche with more opportunity. [L0506.001]
3. Build the Shopify store, including a demo store with uploaded demo products, before contacting suppliers. [L0506.001]
4. Find suppliers and get approved as an authorized retailer through direct outreach using phone and email scripts, without paying for supplier access or supplier directories. [L0506.001]
5. Optimize the site for conversions once real products are live and before spending on paid traffic. [L0506.001]
6. Bring in traffic through paid ads and other traffic sources once the site converts. [L0506.001]
7. Automate and outsource the operation so the business can be managed in about 15 to 30 minutes per day. [L0506.001]

Source A (undated): the program uses a seven-step process for launching each new highly profitable semi-automated store, and niche selection is step one. [L0591.007]

Source B, high-ticket dropshipping (2021-22, older), five-step launch sequence:

1. Identify your market: a customer group with a shared interest that has several viable high-ticket products. [L0458.058]
2. Build a professional Shopify store before contacting any supplier. [L0458.058]
3. Find local suppliers and secure their approval to sell their products. [L0458.058]
4. Load the supplier product data, tidy the design and optimize the product pages for conversion. [L0458.058]
5. Launch with a traffic system built on Google Shopping ads, organic search, email and retargeting. [L0458.058]

Source B (2021-22, older), seven-step full business build:

1. Identify the market and the profitable products to sell. [L0912.005]
2. Build an ecommerce site step by step on Shopify. [L0912.005]
3. Get suppliers to approve you to sell their products through your website. [L0912.005]
4. Get the approved supplier products onto your website. [L0912.005]
5. Launch the business and set up marketing to drive traffic and sales. [L0912.005]
6. Manage the day-to-day operations. [L0912.005]
7. Learn the financial numbers behind running the business. [L0912.005]

Source B (2021-22, older): the steps that must be finished before a high-ticket store can go live are building the website, securing supplier approval and launching the store. Managing day-to-day operations and learning the financial numbers can follow after launch without delaying it. [L0912.013]

Source D (2026), typical sequence for an AI-assisted dropshipping business:

1. Set the mindset, choose the tools and set up the store. [L1487.007]
2. Do product research to find products with winning potential. [L1487.007]
3. Build the store: product listings, branding and conversion optimization. [L1487.007]
4. Launch paid ads and optimize them with AI-assisted targeting. [L1487.007]
5. Scale the store once it is profitable. [L1487.007]

### Launch criteria

**Definition of launched**

- A store launch means officially opening for business to start receiving traffic, sales, questions and data. It is not a one-day event that brings in large cash flow, and it differs from the big launches of established brands. [L1446.004]
- A new dropshipping store counts as launched only when it is live, connected to working suppliers, receiving traffic and ready to take its first sale. Use these conditions as the finish line for the initial build. [L0555.008]
- A store is ready to launch once the first two suppliers are onboarded and their products are uploaded. That is enough to open for business, accept payments, start small-budget ads and begin learning how the business works. [L1446.005]
- Launch as soon as the store is optimized for conversions and has no glaring problems. Further improvements can continue after launch, since the store keeps improving over the life of the business. [L1446.006]

**Supplier count**

Sources differ on the minimum number of suppliers. Each value applies in the situation its source describes:

| Source | Minimum or rule | When it applies | Status | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Source A, 2019 | Some approved suppliers and products | Launch, with more added after launch | Older | [L1068.005] |
| Source A, 2019 | Top brands in the niche first when weekly hours are limited | Limited hours | Older | [L1068.006] |
| Source A, 2020 | One approved supplier | Small first budget in a niche | Older | [L0664.082] |
| Source A, 2021 | Two verified suppliers | Turning on traffic | Older | [L0671.033] |
| Source A, 2021 | At least two brands live | Before turning on traffic; use free time to get the rest approved | Older | [L0671.034] |
| Source A, 2022 | First two brands | Start selling | Older | [L0963.119] |
| Source A, 2023 | At least two approved suppliers live | Launch |, | [L0970.013] |
| Source A, 2023 | Fewer suppliers | List all products in free Google Shopping; paid ads only on the highest-margin products from approved brands |, | [L0970.014] |
| Source A, 2023 | Whatever number is in hand: two, five or ten | Launch day; do not hold off for a fixed target |, | [L1114.011] |

- Launching with one supplier is acceptable for a small first budget, but the 2021 rule says not to start traffic with only one brand. Both are older advice; the 2021 rule is the more specific one for turning on traffic. [L0664.082, L0671.034]
- Do not wait for every supplier to be approved before running ads. [L0664.082]
- Keep sourcing more suppliers every day instead of waiting for sales to come in. [L0671.033]
- Keep adding suppliers in the background while traffic, sales and optimization run. [L0970.013]

**Product count**

- For Google ads, the store should have at least two approved brands and a minimum of about 100 different products listed. Variants of the same product do not count toward the 100. [L0468.006] (older, 2020)
- In 2022, the view was that a big catalogue is not needed at launch because the store builds out over time. The source does not state whether the 100-product rule applies to launch-day sales. Use the 2020 rule for Google ads and the 2022 view for the first sales. [L0963.119, L0468.006]
- Baseline product count for a new store: 100 unique products. [L0823.012]

**Traffic and sales baselines**

- Baseline traffic for a new store: 60 unique clicks per day. [L0823.013]
- Baseline sales for a new store: 1 sale every other day, which is about 0.5 sales per day. [L0823.014]
- Starting baseline, in order (Source A, undated):
  1. Launch the store and run PLDA ads. [L0823.011]
  2. Test the checkout by completing a sale. [L0823.011]
  3. Have 100 unique products. [L0823.011]
  4. Reach 60 unique clicks per day. [L0823.011]
  5. Aim for 1 sale every other day. [L0823.011]

**Avoiding a shortcut**

- Do not build a single-product sales page as a shortcut to conversions. Without a track record and a catalogue, the store cannot carry paid traffic. [L0468.007] (older, 2020)

**Pre-launch review**

- Run a full pre-launch review before switching on paid traffic, because each missing setup item can cost sales and raises the risk of account suspension. A store can be published without being ready, so this review separates a live site from one that converts visitors. [L1145.001] (older, 2021-22)
- A site is technically live once published, but meaningful customer volume starts when the paid campaigns are switched on. Plan the final check around that moment. [L1145.002] (older, 2021-22)

### Hours required

- The model works as a lifestyle business, so launch speed depends on how many hours the owner can put in each week. Someone working a few hours a week needs much longer, while someone working evenings and weekends can be live in about two weeks. [L1068.001] (older, 2019)
- The estimated total effort to finish the initial store setup is roughly 40 to 80 hours, depending on speed and previous experience. This is an estimate, not a fixed figure. [L1068.002] (older, 2019)
- Building the store requires about one hour a day, or several hours on each weekend day. A 21-day launch plan is unrealistic if only a few hours per week are available. [L1068.003] (older, 2019)
- When weekly hours are limited, put the time first into getting approved with the top brands in the niche, uploading their products and setting up traffic campaigns. Less important brands can follow later. [L1068.006] (older, 2019)
- The time from starting training to a live store varies widely: about one week up to about 60 days, depending on the owner's pace. [L0356.014] (older, 2019)

### Time to results

| Stage | Target time | Source | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Product selection to launch of a minimal first test store | At least two days | Source D, 2026 | [L0600.006] |
| First product live, with research, setup and initial ad launch | End of week one | Source D, 2026 | [L1487.001] |
| Build phase, from identified products to live store, if moving quickly | Two to three weeks | Source B, 2021-22 (older) | [L0912.006] |
| Full initial setup for an evenings-and-weekends owner | About two weeks | Source A, 2019 (older) | [L1068.001] |
| Whole path, from training start to live store | About one week to about 60 days | Source A, 2019 (older) | [L0356.014] |

- Product selection to launch can take as little as two days for a first test, because a first test needs a live store that can produce data, not a polished product page. [L0600.006]
- Results depend on the pace of the owner; the table above shows the range from the sources. [L0356.014, L1068.001]

### Milestones, months one to three

| Milestone | Target | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| End of week one | Live store with its first product listed; research, setup and initial ad launch finished | [L1487.001] |
| End of month one | At least one day with 500 to 1,000 USD in revenue; shows real traction, not just a live store | [L1487.002] |
| End of month two | Daily orders and about 10,000 USD monthly revenue (about 333 USD per day) | [L1487.003] |
| End of month three | At least 1,000 USD per day on average, about 30,000 USD per month; consistency over several weeks matters more than one strong day | [L1487.005] |

- A foundation-stage store should have a branded, conversion-optimized Shopify store, a repeatable product research method, ad creatives and copy tested with AI, and a process that can be scaled or handed to someone else. Together these give the data needed to know what is working and why. [L1487.006]

### 21-day launch plan

- Source A (2020) lists a 21-day launch plan as a structured plan for launching a store over three weeks. The December 2020 coaching call names it as a resource, but the lesson does not list its daily tasks. [L0426.015]
- Key facts of the plan (older, 2020):
  1. Repeatable at any time; restart from the members area "Bonuses" section. [L0268.062]
  2. Guides you through the blueprint modules daily. [L0268.062]
  3. Covers web design, store setup and supplier contact within 21 days. [L0268.062]
  4. Alternative: a 7-week launch plan is being added for those who need a slower timeline. [L0268.062]
  5. Both plans are available in the members area at no additional cost. [L0268.062]
- A 21-day plan is unrealistic with only a few hours per week. Building the store needs about one hour a day, or several hours on each weekend day. Use the 7-week alternative if the time available is limited. [L1068.003, L0268.062]

### Launch order

**Ordered launch steps (Source A, 2024)**

1. Complete the compliance checklist. [L0471.062]
2. Set up Google Merchant Center and a Google Shopping campaign. [L0471.062]
3. Set up Google remarketing campaigns. [L0471.062]
4. Add a small Microsoft campaign. [L0471.062]
5. Launch Meta ads under the same compliance rules. [L0471.062]

- Do not start traffic sources before completing the compliance checklist (lesson 6.0.2). [L0969.102] (older, 2022)
- The 2024 order and the 2022 rule agree: compliance comes before any traffic source. [L0471.062, L0969.102]

**Launch in the high-ticket model (Source B, 2021-22, older)**

- Choose the market, the product types and the rough target customer before building anything. [L1060.001]
- Build the demo website before contacting suppliers. It should be nearly identical to the store customers will eventually see, and the main change before launch is adding products from approved suppliers. [L1060.005]
- Before launching a product, choose the entry route in advance: a marketplace such as Amazon first, your own website first, or a crowdfunding campaign such as Kickstarter. Smaller products especially need a written launch plan instead of the assumption that the product will sell by itself. [L0768.001]

### Store build principles for testing

**Speed over polish**

- Copying proven elements from successful competitors is the fastest way to get a test store live, especially for beginners without design experience. Their choices have already been tested by real sales. [L0600.004]
- Building from scratch without studying competitors slows the launch and forces untested design and copy decisions. Beginners who avoid inspiration often lose weeks before they can start testing a product. [L0600.005]
- For a first test, spend little time polishing the product page, because its design matters less than getting a live store that can produce data. [L0600.006]
- In the early testing stage, create a logo fast just to get the store live and the first sale. The full brand work (extensive brand document, customer avatars, unique packaging) comes later, once a product has proven it sells. [L0954.001]
- Choose the store's own theme by previewing it as a draft in the theme library before making it live. [L0971.079]

**Demo store and catalogue**

- Build the Shopify store, including a demo store with uploaded demo products, before contacting suppliers. [L0506.001]
- Start selling after the first two brands are uploaded, and keep adding suppliers in the background. [L0963.119] (older, 2022)

**Entry route for small physical products (Source B, 2021-22, older)**

- For small physical products, either build an on-site sales path in which customers are upsold from cheaper items, or use Amazon, Etsy, eBay or Walmart as a lead magnet to generate traffic. Amazon is named as the most likely option, since it captures a large share of online traffic for physical products. [L0768.019]

### General store testing

**Test order before traffic (Source C)**

Set up a Shopify store with a dummy test order before launch:

1. Place a test order on the live store. [L0352.054]
2. Confirm the purchase flow, Apple Pay and payment steps work. [L0352.054]
3. Check the order flow before sending traffic from TikTok. [L0352.054]

**Conversion check before paid spend**

- Optimize the site for conversions once real products are live and before spending on paid traffic. [L0506.001]
- Launch as soon as the store is optimized for conversions and has no glaring problems. [L1446.006]
- Run the full pre-launch review before switching on paid traffic, and plan the final check around the moment the paid campaigns start. [L1145.001, L1145.002] (older, 2021-22)

#### Further details

- Building and launching a first high-ticket dropshipping business follows a five-step system that runs from setting up the business and launching the store until the first sales come in. Growth and marketing systems come only after that point, in a separate phase. [L0438.014]

## Suppliers, brands and catalog depth

### Demo store and readiness before outreach

- Create 10 to 20 demo products before contacting suppliers, because suppliers will not work with a store they cannot picture on the site. [L0036.001]
- Demo products are placeholder products with AI-generated images, names and descriptions. Suppliers see them to judge what the store will look like before you ask for authorization, which raises your chances of becoming an authorized retailer. [L0088.001]
- Build the website first and then pursue supplier approvals, because approvals and supplier relationships depend on a working store. Plan the build for the next few months rather than waiting for a perfect launch. [L1287.004]

### Becoming an authorized retailer

- Authorization requires that you first present yourself as an official business entity with professional infrastructure: official business registration, a professional business email and a separate business phone. It also requires a proven supplier contact strategy before approval becomes possible. [L0109.004]
- The aim of supplier outreach is approval with about five suppliers, so that you are an authorized retailer for five brands in your niche. The approval is the milestone that matters before any live product sales. [L1063.001]
- The goal is approval with every supplier in the industry, not celebrating the first approvals. Keep contacting unapproved brands every day and follow up with brands already contacted. [L0962.079]
- Non-native English speakers should not hesitate to contact suppliers. Several successful Source A members are non-native speakers with seven-figure businesses, and most rejections are simply one supplier among many. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0268.064]
- Age should not limit you. Representing yourself professionally, including having a phone number, establishes legitimacy regardless of how young you sound. For high-ticket products the customer's concern is business credibility, not age. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0268.065]

### Finding and approaching suppliers

#### How many suppliers to list

- Find at least 20 dropship-friendly suppliers for a niche before committing to it, because only a few will approve in the first days. Even if only about 5 approve at the start, the remaining prospects are followed up later. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0468.003]
- Treat supplier count as a numbers game early on. Many prospects decline or stay silent, so a long list keeps momentum going. Treat rejections as data and return to the other prospects later. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0468.004]
- Pick one product type and source many units of it from many suppliers rather than relying on a single supplier. This spreads supply risk across the catalogue. (Source A, 2023.) [L0934.021]

#### Where to find prospects

1. Open the websites of your top competitors. (Source A, 2019; older advice.) [L1068.007]
2. Go to their bestseller pages. [L1068.007]
3. Note which suppliers make those bestselling products. [L1068.007]
4. Contact those suppliers first. [L1068.007]

- A business owner with an existing physical store can open an ecommerce store next to it and use existing supplier relationships as a competitive advantage. Online-only sellers usually cannot access those relationships. (Source A, 2019; older advice.) [L1068.012]
- If a niche has too few suppliers, broaden to a related category with more product types, for example from rocking chairs to outdoor furniture. This increases the number of brands you can get approved with. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0468.010]
- Brands sold only through physical stores do not count as future competitors or suppliers for the drop-ship model. Look for online-only brands with no warehouse or showroom. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0468.017]
- Selling a product type that is also sold in local retail stores is acceptable, as long as the brands you contact primarily sell online. Brands that depend on a showroom or physical stores are not suitable suppliers. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0468.016]
- Local suppliers hold stock in the customer's own country, so shipping is typically 1 to 7 days, the products are already sold and tested in that market, and the brands are known to buyers. Stock in the customer's country is a fixed requirement: only work with suppliers who already sell in the same country as the customers. (Source B, 2021 to 2022; older advice.) [L0771.005]

#### What to ask for

- Ask the supplier for a current product catalogue and review it before making an offer, because the best sourcing decisions depend on the full list. Many suppliers list products across unrelated niches, so filter those out before you start. [L0965.084]
- When a supplier asks for annual sales goals for a brand-new store, a reasonable answer is a figure between 2 and 500,000 (the currency was not stated). (Source A, 2023.) [L0934.088]

### Supplier fit: margins and pricing

- A supplier with a sliding-scale margin, where the margin depends on sales volume, is not automatically disqualified. Lower-margin products can still be listed, but they may be hard to run ads profitably on. (Source A, 2024.) [L0666.047]
- Pivot question: if a store has about 10 supplier approvals but low margins, keep the store live, upload some low-margin products without paid ads for credibility, and keep reaching out to higher-margin suppliers. Do not pivot completely. (Source A, 2024.) [L0856.115]
- Operating in a country with no MAP policy means price matching cannot be relied on, so pricing must be set from wholesale costs and competitor data. (Source A, 2020; older advice.) [L0967.090]
- Tariffs or supplier cost increases raise wholesale prices, which raises MAP and therefore customer prices. The effect applies to all competitors at the same time, so no single store loses sales to another. (Source A, 2018.) [L1109.035]

#### Case: a MAP-priced store with weak early results

A store owner spent a month building a store where items priced between $1,000 and $2,000 MAP had an average margin of about 20%, and the first week of ads produced one sale. The advice was not to switch niches. Instead, add more approved suppliers first, because about 80% of sales often come from about 20% of products. Until then, cut wasted ad spend using the optimization steps, and spend early money on clicks that bring quality traffic while testing keywords and price points. (Source A, 2023.) [L0835.003]

### Adding value and relationship building

- A supplier relationship depends on you adding value to their business beyond transactional order fulfilment. This is essential for building trust with silver and gold suppliers. (Source A.) [L0121.003]
- Relationship-building tactics such as gifts, insider access and negotiated pricing belong to a later stage: roughly three to six months, or up to a year, after the business starts. They do not belong in the pre-launch phase or the first weeks after the store goes live. (Source A.) [L0939.001]

### Early traffic and brand choice

- Early traffic is easiest to get from brands and products people already search for on Google and Google Shopping. Smaller, unknown brands are best added later, when the store already has traffic, an email list and a social following to promote them. (Source A, 2023.) [L1114.005]

### Catalogue size and expansion

#### Why supplier count matters

- Adding more authorized suppliers and products is one of the two biggest levers for profit and revenue, the other being traffic growth. Stores with 20 to 40 brands have seen one additional supplier add 30 to 50% to revenue, and the impact of a single brand is hard to predict. (Source A.) [L0938.001]
- The two main drivers of store size are supplier approvals for strong brands with in-demand products, and sufficient traffic from ads and SEO. Market size sets the ceiling for how big a store can grow. (Source A, 2025.) [L1287.003]
