- Master Plan monthly coaching gives a competitive advantage by providing business-specific, market-specific and product-specific guidance. It deepens general program content without replacing core instruction. *(older advice, 2021-22)* [L0035.005]
- Master Plan 12-month coaching is appropriate for entrepreneurs in the build-toward-launch phase, or for established operators seeking structured growth guidance over a defined period. *(older advice, 2021-22)* [L0035.006]
- Coaching calls can save large amounts of time, money and energy, because you can learn from other operators' mistakes. Treat them as a core business resource rather than an optional extra. [L1441.005]
- Many dropshipping courses sell pre-recorded videos plus a dead community, so students hit a wall when applying the material alone. A paid program should provide live expert review at each major decision point, not only theory. [L1452.001]
- Use a mentor or course coach to review the product, offer and targeting before scaling. A mentor who knows the product can provide better guidance on audience choice. [L0678.040]

### Avoiding scammers

- Do not respond to cold emails from Shopify "experts" offering design, SEO or marketing services. These companies use volume models to contact all new Shopify stores. Delete the emails. If you need help, contact official Source A support or use vetted service providers. [L0253.045]
- Unsolicited direct messages in online communities, especially ones asking for money, login details or your store's results, are a common scam pattern. Do not share account information or pay anyone who contacts you first. [L1323.011]

### AI tools

- ChatGPT Plus unlocks plugins, web access and more capable models, which the basic free version lacks. Plus costs around 20 dollars per month and is worth considering once automation tools such as Zapier are used. [L0353.014]
- Web browsing in ChatGPT may be temporarily disabled, and the model's knowledge may only reach back to 2021. Check current news and market information with a live search source. [L0353.015]
- AI tools help with brainstorming, brand name ideas, graphic mockups for team review and drafting email templates. Supplier relationships and direct customer relationships still need people, because the trust they build over time cannot be replaced by a tool. [L0857.073]
- AI tools can speed up product research, generate product descriptions and ad copy, and support ad targeting. They reduce guesswork and manual hours but do not replace the operator's own decisions. [L1487.008]

## Planning, vision, goals and operator mindset

### Backwards planning and the destination

- Plan the business by starting from the 10-year end-point and working backwards to the current position. Then place checkpoints along the way to that destination. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0439.003]
- Planning is easier when you start from the target and work backwards, because the steps between today and the target can then be derived. Working forward from the current position without a defined destination is harder. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1256.005]
- Plan backwards from the target in this order: [L1256.006]
  1. Define the end-point target, such as monthly revenue, yearly revenue, or yearly profit. [L1256.006]
  2. Work backwards from that target to identify the intermediate metrics and benchmarks that must be reached along the way. [L1256.006]
  3. Arrange these benchmarks as progressive steps and determine what each step requires. [L1256.006]
- A target such as $10 million in annual revenue is too abstract to plan for directly when starting out. Set the destination first, then break it down into the milestones needed to reach it. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1256.007]
- Break a revenue goal into milestones, such as targets two months and four months ahead, then list the actions needed in the next few weeks. Treat this as a simple business plan for the launch. (Older advice, Source A 2021.) [L0962.080]
- Worked planning example: a store with $50,000 monthly revenue (about $600,000 per year) that needs to reach $3 million in year three can set $1 million as the year-one target, $2 million for year two, and $3 million for year three. This means adding roughly $1 million of revenue each year. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1519.006]
- Detailed strategy and tactics become practical only when the target is three years or less away. A 10-year vision works best as a destination that is broken into 3-year and 1-year milestones. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1519.018]

### Ten-year vision

- Set the vision questions in this order before writing the vision document: (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0439.010]
  1. How much revenue will the business produce per year? [L0439.010]
  2. What profit margin, and what profit per year, will it make on that revenue? [L0439.010]
  3. Which products will be sold? [L0439.010]
  4. What will the website look like? [L0439.010]
  5. How will customers be engaged? [L0439.010]
  6. What will the marketing look like? [L0439.010]
  7. How big will the team need to be? (for example 5, 10 or 20 people) [L0439.010]
  8. How involved will the owner be day to day: still working in the business, or in an advisory or management role? [L0439.010]
  9. What will the owner's day-to-day life look like? [L0439.010]
  10. What personal income and personal financial situation does the owner want? [L0439.010]
  11. How will the business finances be managed? [L0439.010]
  12. How well known will the brand be, and who will the customers be? [L0439.010]
  13. What are the 3 to 5 core values? [L0439.010]
- Describe the traffic mix in 10 years, for example a shift from heavy paid traffic from Google Ads or Facebook ads toward referrals and organic traffic. Alternatively, paid ads can be maximized if they stay profitable. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0439.018]
- A vision can include selling the business within 10 years. In that case, the owner should describe what business or work they want to do afterwards, because the vision still needs a plan beyond the current company. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0439.009]
- Think big-picture about doubling a business instead of focusing on small monthly gains. Ask what could double revenue overnight, and plan for those large levers, such as major retail distribution, a new clinical or patent asset, a direct-response TV or radio campaign, or a new sales platform. [L0334.049]
- A long-term goal can be a brand that retires the owner over a four to five year period by building revenue and then selling it. [L1212.047]

### Documenting goals and vision

- Document goals and vision as follows: (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0414.014]
  1. Write a documented set of goals and a vision covering the next month, the next year, three years out, and beyond. [L0414.014]
  2. Treat this document as the roadmap that all marketing work is planned against. [L0414.014]
  3. Do this before building the marketing system, because skipping it leads to a lack of focus and weaker results. [L0414.014]
- Define a specific income target in your currency before choosing a business model or strategy. The target determines how much profit per sale and how many orders are needed to reach it. [L1503.009]

### 180-day plans and goal setting

- Build the 180-day plan in this order: (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0447.006]
  1. Copy the Google Sheet planner into your own Google Drive. Never edit the shared original. [L0447.006]
  2. Enter the plan end date, which is also the next review date. [L0447.006]
  3. Set revenue goals for the six months: a total, a monthly target such as by end of June or July, or a weekly figure. [L0447.006]
  4. Set profit goals, either keeping the current profit margin or raising it if the past year showed the need. [L0447.006]
  5. Set no more than five goals or priorities. Fewer is fine. [L0447.006]
  6. For each priority, set measurables with target numbers at the six-month mark. [L0447.006]
  7. Write a short description of the business and life as they will look when the goals are reached. [L0447.006]
  8. For each priority, list every task needed, including recurring weekly tasks. [L0447.006]
  9. Break the plan into two 90-day quarters (first and second quarter rocks), with 90-day priorities and tasks that are smaller steps toward the six-month targets. [L0447.006]
- Set revenue and profit targets that are realistic for the six-month window, because unrealistic short-term targets are demotivating. In high-ticket dropshipping, a business doing $50,000 per month cannot realistically reach $1,000,000 per month within six months, but reaching $100,000 per month in six months is achievable. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0447.008]
- Set long-term goals (10-year and 3-year) very ambitiously, because people overestimate short-term progress and underestimate long-term progress. Goals under one year should be more realistic. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0447.009]
- Expect a new dropshipping business to need a planned first phase before the next stage. Use the 7-day and 60-day plans as short-term steps and the 12-month plan as the long-term effort. With AI, the 7-day and 60-day plans can be drafted in a few hours, while the 12-month plan is a long-term effort. This assumes the site audit and its fixes are already done before the planning starts. [L1389.001]

### Annual review of fundamentals

- Review and update the core marketing and business fundamentals at least once every 12 months, whether the business is new or several years old. Anything never done before must be completed for the first time. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0761.001]
- Some fundamentals should be checked more often than once a year. Competitor analysis, business figures and goals change over time and must be kept current, so they need the most frequent review. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0761.002]

### Focus on one business

- Do not overcomplicate the dropshipping business model. Focus on the fundamentals: find the right product, build a basic store, run ads, collect emails, and scale. [L0280.043]
- At this stage, run one niche and one store at a time. Do not start several stores in parallel. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0490.025]
- Splitting effort across several parallel ideas produces weaker results than putting 100% of the effort into one business model. [L0631.002]
- Focus on one business at a time. Running several projects at once divided attention and slowed growth. Cutting side projects to concentrate on the one with the highest revenue potential was the decision that let growth accelerate. [L0790.006]
- Improving one strategy incrementally is usually more effective than switching strategies altogether when a current approach shows partial results. [L0691.030]
- Not every advanced strategy suits every store. Choose which levers to pull based on what the store's own numbers show it needs. [L0544.002]
- Tactics that work for one operator may not work in another niche, so test each one in your own business before relying on it. [L1357.046]
- Make the most of what you have to start. Build the store with the resources you have, and keep improving rather than waiting for ideal conditions or premium tools. (Older advice, Source A 2021.) [L0801.169]
- Early in the business, focus on the tasks that bring the next level of revenue, such as adding products and suppliers and running ads. Skip polish and low-impact projects until the store is generating sales. (Older advice, Source A 2022.) [L1281.029]
- Once a store works, the work is continuous improvement, not switching niches. Keep reviewing operations, supplier quality, product pages and ad efficiency. (Older advice, Source A 2021.) [L0853.070]
- Drop shipping is a long-term business that depends on continuing to improve. Keep auditing ad accounts, adding suppliers and expanding into new niches over time. (Older advice, Source A 2021.) [L0801.173]
- No online store is fully passive, because real products, customers, and suppliers all need attention. Income can be semi-passive but still requires regular oversight. [L0826.018]
- When starting a new project next to an existing business, use a 90/10 time and investment split. Keep 90% on the proven business or career and 10% on the new venture, so the proven income is not harmed while the new idea can grow. (Source A 2024.) [L0805.051]
- If you cannot change the game you play, change the rules you play by. If you can do neither, you are probably in the wrong market. [L0378.011]
- Product testing done only to read metrics carries low emotional risk, which is why many sellers stay stuck in testing indefinitely. Start a brand with the intention of building a real business that can be sold later. [L1042.011]

### Realistic timelines and patience

- Accept that there is no reliable timeline for reaching a specific monthly profit, because many variables affect results. The priority is to build consistent traffic and sales each month rather than waiting for sales to arrive by chance. (Older advice, Source A 2019.) [L0960.058]
- Progress builds up in small steps, for example zero to one, then one to two, then two to three. Constant small progress reaches a large goal. Do not let time pass without taking action. (Older advice, Source A.) [L0359.008]
- Build the business with a horizon of about 12 months rather than a few months. Businesses that make the most money are often the ones that stay around the longest. [L1251.009]
- Expect at least six months before a new brand shows a clear picture of whether it can succeed. Do not treat the first months as the verdict on the business. [L1508.023]
- Expect a new dropshipping business to take six months to one year, or longer, with paid guidance, before results are consistent. Review each failure with the question why it failed, and let each failure yield concrete takeaways. [L1440.002]
- Expect one to two years before the brand really gets going. The brand was founded in 2017, but full-scale operation only started around 2019, so plan for a long, slow build with a lot of trial and error. [L1438.003]
- Build the business around a long-term vision and keep moving toward it in steps, rather than trying to force fast results. Patience combined with a steady income is what lets a founder survive the early failures. [L1438.004]

The timeline figures measure different things and do not contradict each other. Six months is the minimum before a clear verdict, six months to one year or longer is the range for consistent results with paid guidance, and one to two years is the range for the brand to get going at full scale. [L1508.023, L1440.002, L1438.003]

### Income floor and steady income

- Keep a fixed monthly income floor from a small, low-hour job while the store is still unstable, because dropshipping revenue can vary sharply from month to month. This lets the owner cover bills and avoid being forced to make business decisions out of financial pressure. [L0690.004]
- Decide in advance on a minimum monthly amount the business must reliably reach. Go full-time only once that minimum can be hit comfortably every month. [L0690.006]
- Keep cash reserves and be ready to start a new store or product at any time, because a single store can collapse quickly. Treat the current winner as temporary and keep building the next test in parallel. Running several products or a second store at once reduces the anxiety caused by depending on one winner that may fade. [L0690.005, L0690.044]
- Keeping a steady income source while the business is young gives you financial stability and freedom to experiment without pressure. The job can also provide skills, contacts and a daily routine that help the side business. [L1343.029]
- While a new business is still small, keep a steady income source, and choose that job for transferable skills such as research, web design or copywriting. The income protects you from financial pressure, and the skills can be applied directly to the side business. [L1343.045]
- A business owner can increase income in steps by growing profit, for example from $3,000 to $4,000 or from $7,000 to $8,000 per month. An employee rarely gets a raise of $1,000 per month, so business ownership is the more realistic path to that kind of income increase. [L1126.011]

### Lifestyle versus corporate choices

- A typical lifestyle drop shipping store reaches about $10,000 to $60,000 in monthly sales and $4,000 to $10,000 in monthly net profit, with the owner working around 15 hours per week. [L0413.001]
- A lifestyle business is built to return the most money for the least owner time invested, so time cost should be weighed alongside revenue when choosing a business model. [L0585.001]
- For a lifestyle business, aim for the smallest input of effort and time that produces the largest output of results, profit and scalability. Choose niches with this ratio in mind from the start. [L1517.001]
- Decide early whether you want a lifestyle business that gives you location freedom, or a larger corporate-style company with employees and many locations. Both are realistic, and the choice determines where your effort and energy go. [L1039.005]
- Online businesses selling physical products without holding stock let the operator work from almost any location and travel while running the store. This location freedom is one of the main reasons to choose e-commerce over a local business. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1014.004]
- Early on, working for a small owner-operated business, even outside e-commerce, teaches management, operations and entrepreneurial habits. These skills matter most later, when staff handle day-to-day work and the owner oversees them. (Older advice, Source A 2020.) [L0852.002]
- Typical life-design targets for a drop-shipping owner, starting point versus goal: [L0827.002]

| Area | Starting point | Target |
|---|---|---|
| Monthly expenses | $4,000-5,000 | $1,000-2,000 |
| Tax burden | 40%+ | around 5% |
| Weekly working hours | 40+ | under 20 |
| Responsibilities | many | almost none |
| Vacations | one every few years | more frequent |
| Monthly savings | $200 | $3,000-8,000 |

[L0827.002]

### Business as an asset

- Build the business as an asset that keeps running without the owner's daily work, because buyers pay for self-running businesses and not for a job. Cash flows from short-lived trends are worth less to buyers than lasting assets. [L1359.001]
- Test whether a business is an asset by checking whether it keeps running and earning while the owner stays away for one to two months. If income stops when the owner stops working, the business is a job, and buyers will value it as one. [L1359.045]
- Drop shipping and online sales generated nearly all income, while offline businesses took far too much time for too little money. Offline businesses were sold off once online operations were established. (Older advice, Source A 2020.) [L0852.009]
- Once a repeatable process works, the same steps can be applied to build another store. This makes losing a store less threatening, because the process itself is the valuable asset. [L0826.021]
- Once a business generates money, you can reinvest in a second opportunity, build multiple income streams, or keep the one that gives you energy. A dropshipping store can also serve as a base to pivot into work you actually want to do. [L0701.029]
- Case: a seller made printed t-shirts for many hobby audiences (scuba diving, snowboarding, skating) on the platform Teespring and had some results, such as about 50 sales for some designs and a couple of shares for others. He struggled to see why he cared about the shirts beyond needing the money, and other changes in his life pushed him to decide on a change. He first worked on personality and found he already knew his proficiencies well, but his problem was matching his self-confidence and choosing the market he wanted to serve. His values were connecting people and building communities, and helping others master their craft. He then moved to hobby t-shirts for older women, covering anything a grandmother or aunt does in her free time, and set up a Facebook group where sewers and knitters share patterns and techniques. He set up the group but did not look at it for about two weeks, because a virtual assistant (VA) manages it, and he uses a full-time designer for the designs. He still builds the analytical framework that defines target audiences and design direction, and an assistant manages the budgets, drawing on his background in PPC consulting. Working with this setup, the overall business grew about three times, and he described the change as going from pushing a rock uphill for three to four months to feeling gravity help him down once he understood himself. [L0701.030]

### Anti-fragility and resilience

- Anti-fragility is an ongoing process, not a fixed state. Turn every negative situation into a chance to find order, and build a repeatable process that gains value from each setback. [L1357.039]
- A first store that does not perform is not doomed and should not be discarded automatically. Use what you learn to improve it, and build another store alongside it rather than instead of it. (Older advice, Source A 2020.) [L0961.077]
- In business, reward generally rises with the risk taken, so a conservative approach usually produces small returns. Check your actual risk level by looking at your bank account and the commitments you have made. Think big, because building a large business requires about the same effort as building a small one. [L1513.018]

### Advertising principles for testing

- Treat advertising as a measurable sales investment, and judge each campaign by the sales and profit it actually produces, not by brand awareness or general exposure. Any spend that cannot be tied to results is a guess and should be justified or cut. [L1261.001]
- Advertising is salesmanship at scale. Hold each ad to the standard of a good salesperson who must justify their time and cost. One ad reaches thousands, so a weak ad costs far more than a weak sales conversation. [L1261.002]
- Only scale a channel, offer or ad once a small, traced test has shown the cost per customer and the profit per customer. Methods that consistently win in tests become standard rules for the business. [L1261.003]
- The cost per customer measured in a small test is a reliable predictor for a large rollout, because averages hold when the test is representative. Knowing what the first thousand customers cost tells you what the next million will cost. [L1261.004]
- Do not start national or wide advertising before the product can actually be found and bought by the people who respond. An ad that sends demand to places where the product is not available wastes its budget. [L1261.005]
- Spending money to educate the whole market on a new habit usually costs far more than the sales it creates, and competitors often capture the benefit. Campaigns should target people who already have the need and are open to buying a solution. [L1261.006]
- In one test, a habit-conversion campaign cost $20 to $25 per converted person, and much of the spend reached people who were already converted. Costs like this make one-time sales unprofitable unless repeat purchases are very likely. [L1261.007]
- Mail-order sales of single products rarely cost less than $42.50 per customer, so a one-off product must be able to recover that cost through its margin or repeat purchases. Compare the expected revenue per customer with the acquisition cost before launching. [L1261.008]
- The fastest path to national distribution often starts with direct response offers that create demand. Retailers then stock the product once customers ask for it, and they are more willing to stock a product when they can see proven demand. [L1261.009]
- Small expenses on a guess rarely produce big gains, while small tested expenses can grow into large profitable campaigns. Scale is safe only when the unit economics have been proven at small size. [L1261.010]
- Mail-order advertising is the most accountable form of advertising, because every reply, sale and cost is visible, so its ads are good models to copy. When a non-direct campaign departs from proven direct response methods, the departure should be deliberate and its extra cost known. [L1261.011]

## Valuing and selling a store

### Deciding whether and when to sell

- Stores are usually sold for a few recurring reasons: the owner has better uses for the cash, a life event happens, the owner has lost interest, or the business has peaked and stopped growing. [L0858.001]
- Common concrete reasons include wanting cash for a second store or another business; life events such as marriage, divorce, bereavement or adoption costs; boredom with the niche; and a growth plateau that a buyer with a different skill set may still push higher. [L0553.002]
- Owners sell for concrete personal reasons such as paying off a mortgage, affording a baby, moving capital into rental property, or funding a larger business. Finding the real reason helps the seller negotiate the terms they actually need. [L1468.001]
- Deciding whether to sell is personal. One option is to sell only some of the stores to build a cash reserve and keep the rest. (older, 2020) [L0468.012]
- Sell when the business has growing or steady sales and profits. Two years of historical data strengthens buyer confidence, and the market trend does not need to be perfect. [L0231.007]
- A flat business is also acceptable to sell. A declining business sells at the low end of the multiple range, and buyers are very nervous about it. [L0554.005]
- Sell while revenue and traffic are steady, slightly declining, or rising, since buyers prefer those profiles. Letting a site decline through neglect makes it much harder to sell and lowers its price. [L1468.003]
- Sell a store while its revenue and profit are rising every month, because buyers pay more for a store on an upward trend. If a store has a weak month, improve it before considering a sale. [L0826.014]
- Do not sell just because a store reached a revenue milestone. Keep feeding a store that is still growing, and consider selling once it flatlines, because spending another 18 months growing it can add a large amount to the eventual sale price. (older, 2020) [L1277.005]
- If growth is still climbing steeply three months before listing, consider delaying the listing until it levels off. [L1468.015]
- Avoid listing at a peak you believe is final. Sellers often close on the expectation that a buyer will keep growing the business, and owners often do not know when they have reached a peak, so buyers may acquire at the top profit level to grow it further. [L0553.025]
- The two positions above differ in emphasis: the 2020 advice (older) says to sell once growth flattens, while the peak warning says not to list when you believe the peak is final. In both, do not wait for the decline. [L1277.005, L0553.025, L1468.003]
- Do not sell right after a peak season. [L0553.039]
- A store is worth closing mainly when its customer service workload outweighs its sales, which is common with low-ticket products. A store with running ads but little traffic should be optimized first, because its conversion rate cannot be judged yet. (2025) [L0837.066]
- Do not turn off ads or stop running the store once it is listed for sale. Buyers want running and growing businesses, so the business must keep performing during the sale process. [L0554.007]
- Do not wait until you are disengaged from the business to sell. An actively managed, profitable operation commands a higher valuation than one run by a burned-out owner. [L0231.008]
- A business run at 20 or more hours per week sells for the lowest multiplier on net profit or annual sales. A business run at five hours per week or less gets a significantly higher multiplier and attracts a larger pool of buyers who want a portfolio asset. [L1122.005]
- A dropshipping site earning 1,000 USD per month that needs about 40 hours of personal work per month is hard to sell, because buyers see it as a job. Preparing a site for sale should start about six months before the sale. [L1359.002]

#### Holding period and minimum ownership

Sources give different holding periods. Each applies to a different situation.

| Holding period | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|
| At least 6 months before relisting a store bought from another owner | General guideline for resale of a bought store | [L0553.024] |
| 4 months | Done in special cases only; not a sound business model, because buyers ask why the store was resold so fast and the 12-month profit average would show only the owner's short period of added value | [L0553.024] |
| About 6 months | Store can be sold if revenue is stable or rising | [L0470.030] |
| 12 months | Store at least one year old sells for the highest valuation multiples; holding 12 months instead of six allows more history and growth, which can raise the multiple; a store sold after 12 months gets a higher multiple than the same store sold after 6 months, even with identical profit | [L0966.002, L0836.047, L0855.079, L1277.006] |
| 12 to 18 months | Proven track record, consistent results and growing sales; described as the way to maximum value (older, 2020) | [L0852.007] |
| At least 2 years | Maximum valuation, because buyers trust longer profit history; one year can still be listed, and older businesses are worth more | [L0554.004] |

- Hold a store for about 12 to 18 months before selling to show a proven track record, consistent results and growing sales. (older, 2020) [L0852.007]
- A store sold after 12 months gets a higher multiple on net profit than the same store sold after 6 months, even when profit is identical. (2023) [L0855.079]
- A store that is at least one year old sells for the highest valuation multiples. Younger stores can still be sold, but at lower multiples. (2025) [L0966.002]
- A store with a weak launch month followed by several strong months still has value. A longer record of stable or growing months lifts the multiple a buyer will pay. (2025) [L0966.003]

## Valuation

#### Formula and pricing basis

- Store valuation = average monthly net profit (revenue minus expenses) × multiple. Online dropshipping stores sold at 21x to 35x monthly net profit, which is roughly 2x to 3x annual profit. [L0554.001]
- Example: a dropshipping store with $5,000 monthly net profit values at $105,000 using a 21x multiple or $175,000 using a 35x multiple. [L0231.003]
- Use the 12-month average of net profit as the pricing basis, because buyers trust longer periods more. A 3- to 6-month average can fit a fast-growing business, but a steady $5,000 per month for 12 months is worth more than $5,000 in only the last month. [L0554.003]
- Buyers who pay high multiples value an online store on its trailing 12 months of revenue and net profit, with the averages of the most recent three months weighted most heavily. (2025) [L0966.001]
- Store value is not created by hours worked, money spent or future potential. Buyers pay only for the profit the store earns now, so owners should first increase profit and then sell. [L0858.003, L0553.040, L0553.001]
- Sellers should focus on maximizing a store's value before listing it, because buyers pay for proven monthly net profit rather than for hours worked, invested money, or future potential. [L0553.001]
- Buyers pay for a fast learning curve, for trading money for time, for established market demand visible in existing sales, and for applying their own unique advantage, such as paid social ad skills, to a store that lacks them. [L0553.003]

#### Multiples by source and period

Sources differ. Use the figure that matches the source's date and the store's profile.

| Source and period | Multiple | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2019 (older) | About 30x monthly net profit | Example: $5,000 per month costs roughly $150,000 | [L1002.001] |
| 2020 to 2022 (older) | About 30x monthly net profit | Example: $5,000 per month = $150,000; also quoted for websites, networks and stores | [L0967.087, L1092.024, L1277.004, L0270.006] |
| 2021 (older) | About 30x; lower if sales are trending down or the store has limited history | Example: $5,000 per month = $150,000 asking price | [L0270.006] |
| 2022 (older) | 23x to 40x, per listings cited in the lesson | See listing table below | [L0458.007] |
| Current range | 21x to 35x monthly net profit (roughly 2x to 3x annual profit) | Low end for declining stores; top end for steady, diversified stores | [L0554.001, L0554.008] |
| Last 12 months (current) | Listing average 27x; sold average 24x | Example: $10,000 monthly profit listed at $270,000, sold at about $240,000 | [L0553.027] |

- The store valuation multiple of about 30x was the general figure for dropship stores in the older guidance, and it fell when sales were trending down or the store had limited history. (older, 2021) [L0270.006]
- The 2019 and 2020 to 2022 guidance (older) put the general multiple at about 30x, and the current range is 21x to 35x. [L1002.001, L0967.087, L0554.001]

#### Listed examples

Listings cited in the Source B lesson (older, 2021-22):

| Niche | Monthly revenue | Monthly net profit | Listing price | Multiple of monthly net profit | Business created |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Outdoors, Home | $165,651 | $21,463 | $858,509 | 40x | April 2016 |
| Food & Beverages, Equipment | $60,418 | $9,625 | $356,110 | 37x | September 2014 |
| Food & Beverages, Outdoors, Survival & Security | $263,495 | $84,208 | $1,936,780 | 23x | January 2012 |

- A high-ticket dropshipping site can be valued as a sellable asset. The listings cited in the lesson priced sites at 23x to 40x monthly net profit. The lesson's own sale of an early dropshipping site netted nearly $200,000 after two years of running it. (older, 2021-22) [L0458.006, L0458.007]
- Listing data shows how such stores are valued and what their numbers look like. Use these listings as a reference instead of guesses about a store's performance. (older, 2022) [L0803.099]
- Empire Flippers listings require accurate financial records. (older, 2021-22) [L0449.011]

#### Valuation of 2014 marketplace sales

- In September 2014, the average sale price on the Empire Flippers marketplace was about $258,000 per site, a figure from the broker's own reporting. Buyers outnumbered good sellers at that time, so finding profitable established sites was the main bottleneck. [L1468.010]
- A broker valued a website with $1,000 monthly net profit at about $20,000, roughly 20x monthly net profit. A strategic buyer could reasonably pay $25,000 to $30,000 for the same site. [L1468.005]

#### Brokers' sales data by year

| Year | Stores sold | Average sale price | Average time to sell |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2017 | 12 (one per month) | About $70,000 | About 2.5 months |
| 2018 | 15 | $138,000 (almost double 2017) | About 2 months |

- The broker rejected about two-thirds of submitted businesses and listed about one-third. Listed dropshipping businesses sold for multiples from 21x to 35x monthly profit over the two years before this talk. [L0554.008]
- In the last 12 months, 26 dropshipping stores sold for a total of $3.03 million, with prices from $30,000 to $533,000 and an average of $116,000. Smaller stores around $30,000 produced about $1,000 monthly profit, and the largest, around $533,000, produced about $20,000 monthly profit. [L0553.028]
- Average time on market in the last 12 months was 66 days, and about 60 percent of the 26 stores sold in under 60 days. Two to three months is a reasonable timeframe. [L0553.027]

#### Profit-based examples

- A store with an AliExpress/apparel model, $8,700 monthly profit, Facebook-only traffic, Chinese shipping and decreasing monthly earnings was listed at $183,000 (21x). It took 126 days to sell and sold for $120,000 (13x). The store was 12 months old, and buyers had the same negative view of AliExpress models. [L0553.029]
- A dropshipping lifestyle store with $5,000 monthly net profit, 18 months old, using Google Ads, organic traffic and Facebook retargeting, with steady earnings, was listed at $142,000 (28x). It sold for $133,000 (26x) in 10 days from listing. [L0553.030]
- Pets niche case, sold through a broker: see the case listings section. [L0554.052]

#### Revenue-based and brand valuation

- The valuation multiple depends on revenue, not profit, so an exit at a revenue multiple can be much higher than a profit multiple. [L1153.014]
- A brand with its own store, email list, Facebook group and subscription service is valued much higher than a pure Amazon seller. An Amazon-only business sold for about 30 to 35 times monthly profit, while a true brand with those assets can reach about 2.5 to 3 times annual revenue. [L1153.012]
- The first brand the course group sold achieved a 37 times multiple on monthly net profit. The sellers note that a revenue multiple of 2.5 times would have produced about double the sale price, which is why they built a brand. [L1153.013]
- A brand with consistent revenue, returning customers and several traffic channels can be sold for about 3x to 5x its profit, even when profit margin is only 10 to 15 percent. Building the brand is therefore an asset, not only a profit source. [L0871.013]
- Sales from a store sold on a verified marketplace are typically valued at about 5 to 7 times annual profit. Empire Flippers was mentioned as a marketplace to check, and the Shopify Exchange marketplace had closed. [L0642.039]
- The 5 to 7 times annual profit figure is higher than the current 2x to 3x annual profit range for dropshipping stores [L0554.001]. Apply it to sales on verified marketplaces only, as stated for that source. [L0642.039, L0554.001]
- A profitable high-ticket dropshipping store may be sold as an asset for anywhere from tens of thousands to around $1 million, typically a couple of years after it is built. Treat these as anecdotal ranges, not a valuation standard. (older, 2021-22) [L0912.003]
- A store or network is typically valued at about 30 times its monthly net profit. A network making 25,000 USD net profit per month would sell for roughly 750,000 USD. (older, 2020) [L1277.004]
- A dropshipping store that sells about 100,000 USD per year is treated as a successful benchmark, and the first sale is an early milestone on the way to it. Stores at that level can be valued well above 100,000 USD when sold. [L0826.024]

## Factors that raise or lower the price

#### Factors that raise the price

- Net profit alone does not set the value of a store; quality matters. A store with diversified traffic, several products and steady profit sells for more than a store with higher profit that depends on one channel and shows declining trends. [L0553.031]
- Higher quality, meaning steady earnings, several traffic sources and a longer track record, produces a higher sale price and a faster sale. Quality matters more for the price than the owner's effort. [L0858.015]
- Buyers want traffic from several sources, because a single source such as Google Ads creates risk if it underperforms. Organic and social traffic alongside paid traffic makes buyers more comfortable. [L0554.009]
- Specialist stores are worth more than broad general stores. Specialist stores can build email lists, Facebook groups and push notification lists that buyers value. [L0554.012]
- A specific niche with a recognizable brand protects valuation, because it differentiates the store from large retailers and creates assets competitors cannot replicate overnight. [L0231.005]
- Store niches with broad consumer demand, such as dropshipping lifestyle niches, sell more quickly and at higher multiples than AliExpress-sourced stores that rely on Chinese shipping, poor product quality and Facebook-only traffic. [L0553.026]
- Dropshipping lifestyle stores sell quickly at high multiples; member stores typically sell quickly at high multiples. (older, 2021) [L0270.007]
- A business with documented, transferable systems is worth substantially more than one that depends on the owner. The lesson claims an increase of several times and sometimes up to about 10x. Systems also make the business easier to hand over. [L1473.035]
- If 30% of your traffic comes from one forum account, buyers will expect that account to be included in the sale. Be ready to explain every major referral source to a buyer. [L1468.009]
- A store with a strong general trend and a long financial history is easier to value, and the seller can explain irregular months. [L0553.053 is not used; see L0554.053 below] [L0554.053]
- Sites in the same niche with overlapping suppliers are valued together, and a buyer will often purchase related sites as a bundle. Keeping them under one LLC makes that bundled sale simpler. (older, 2022) [L1092.023]
- Strategic buyers, usually competitors in the same niche, generally pay the highest price. They can cross-sell to your existing customers, so they value the customer base more than the current profit alone. [L1468.004]
- A brand built around proven sales can later be sold to another owner, so it is worth structuring the store and its records so the business can be transferred. This makes it easier to build several brands one after another. [L1439.056]
- Build a brand that can later be sold: grow revenue with a brand, build infrastructure, connections and branding partnerships, and then sell the whole brand to a larger company. [L1212.046]
- Stores with a full set of SOPs, training documents and documentation are easier to sell. A business with a virtual assistant running daily operations and documented SOPs is a clear example. [L0554.053, L0554.052]

#### Factors that lower the price

- Buyers reject AliExpress-based stores because they lack a defensible moat (easily copied), offer poor customer experience from China shipping, depend entirely on finding winning Facebook ads, and have no established brand. [L0231.004]
- The first question a broker asks when vetting a dropshipping store is whether it is a Source A store or an AliExpress-style store. Brokers stopped listing AliExpress-style stores because they were hard to sell. [L0554.055]
- A store that depends on a single traffic source with falling earnings is hard to sell and sells at a much lower multiple. Diversifying traffic and keeping earnings steady protects the sale price. [L0858.014]
- Buyers pay a lower price for stores with one channel because a single source creates risk. Diversifying traffic and suppliers reduces perceived risk for buyers because the business does not depend on a single channel or vendor. [L0231.009, L0858.014]
- Avoid building stores around short-term fad niches. Buyers prefer sustainable markets with long-term product demand. [L0231.006]
- Monthly dips are normal and do not hurt the sale if the general trend is steady or positive. Each dip should have an explanation, such as a supplier problem, because unexplained, sporadic earnings worry buyers. [L0554.006]
- A heavily seasonal business such as Halloween supplies may receive a lower multiple when sold far outside its season, because buyers must estimate performance. Seasonal stores can still be sold. [L0553.039]
- A store with no profit, weak assets and no email list or social following is worth roughly 1,000 to 3,000 dollars. A store with built-up assets such as an email list and social pages may reach about 4,000 to 5,000 dollars. Profitable stores are valued as a multiple of monthly net profit, not on a design-only basis. [L0941.004]
- Non-English dropshipping or affiliate sites are avoided by buyers, unless the foreign-language portion is small. English-speaking countries pose no problem. [L0553.035]
- Local services alongside ecommerce are not included in the valuation. Establish the local service as a separate legal entity. [L0231.027]
- Only 100% online businesses can be sold through the marketplace. A local service run by the owner is usually not counted in earnings, although a local team that can be managed online may be considered. [L0554.011]
- Letting a site decline through neglect makes it much harder to sell and lowers its price. [L1468.003]
- A broker valued a site and a strategic buyer could pay more; the difference between a broker's valuation and a strategic price is a reason to approach competitors. [L1468.005, L1468.013]

#### Store examples that show the effect of neglect

- A seller had a Twitter-background website earning a couple of thousand dollars of profit per month. The owner stopped working on it, and over a few years profit fell to a couple of hundred dollars per month. At its peak the site could have sold for about $40,000 to $50,000, but it was given away for free instead. [L1468.002]

## Preparing the store for sale

#### Six months before the sale

1. Make your final changes to content, link profile and SEO, including any change of monetization model, because Google needs a couple of months to recognize them. [L1468.014]
2. Start tracking revenue, costs and owner time separately for each store. [L1468.014]
3. Order or run a basic due diligence report to find problems buyers would flag, and fix what you can. [L1468.014]
4. Cut exploratory paid traffic that has no ROI and focus on what produces net profit. [L1468.014]
5. Speed up the site with quick code fixes rather than an expensive hosting upgrade. [L1468.014]
6. Audit the backlink profile, disavow spam links, and reduce over-optimized anchor text by adding more varied anchors. [L1468.014]
7. Replace duplicate product descriptions with unique ones. [L1468.014]
8. Install Google Analytics (or an accepted alternative such as Clicky) and make sure you control or can grant admin access. [L1468.014]

#### Three months before the sale

1. Compare current traffic trend (stable, growing, declining or seasonal) with the changes you made three months earlier and check whether they are still helping. [L1468.015]
2. If growth is still climbing steeply, consider delaying the listing until it levels off. [L1468.015]
3. Identify strategic buyers, get to know competitors and check whether they would be open to acquiring your business. [L1468.015, L1468.013]
4. Check that supplier, affiliate and special pricing terms are clean and can be transferred to a new owner. [L1468.015]
5. Verify that you are visibly a real person online, such as with real social profiles and connections. [L1468.015]

Start looking for strategic buyers and talking to competitors about three months before the sale. Strategic buyers are the route most likely to produce the highest multiple. [L1468.013]

#### One month before the sale

1. Document all processes as standard operating procedures (SOPs). [L1468.016]
2. Replace yourself in day-to-day work with VAs, so the business runs without you. [L1468.016]
3. Review the financials, cut useless expenses, and confirm revenue and profit are clean and tracked per store. [L1468.016]
4. Set up a training and site-turnover plan for the new owner. [L1468.016]
5. Add at least two alternate suppliers, even if you do not use them. [L1468.016]
6. Shop for a broker or platform that fits your site and price target. [L1468.016]
7. Turn off WHOIS privacy so your name shows as the domain owner. [L1468.016]
8. Prepare the listing materials: earnings records, traffic data, and any video or screencast evidence. [L1468.016]
9. Make sure all financial figures in the listing match each other exactly and that date ranges and metric definitions (sessions or page views) are stated. [L1468.016]

#### Sale timeline

- Prepare a sale plan about six months before the sale, because the site needs time to recover from changes. [L1359.002, L1468.014]
- Vetting usually takes 2 to 4 weeks. [L0554.013]
- Typical time on market is about 2 to 3 months. Average time on market in the last 12 months was 66 days. [L0553.027]
- Store listed with a broker: the full timeline is in the broker process below. [L0554.013]

#### Documentation and handover preparation

- When selling an online store, do not leave the handover to the end. Assemble the store's documentation, supplier and account details, and process notes before listing it, because reconstructing them after the sale is slow and frustrating. [L0826.023]
- Keep documentation as a separate asset. A brand built around proven sales with clean records can be transferred to another owner. [L1439.056]
- A transition can be handled by informing each supplier that a new owner is taking over, asking them to remove your card from future payments, and putting the new owner's card on file. The same step applies when closing a store to stop future charges. (2023) [L0970.017]

## Sales channels

#### Overview of channels

| Channel | Advantages | Disadvantages | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| DIY, direct to strategic buyers (competitors) | Usually the highest price; no listing or broker fee | You must open your books (numbers, keywords, paid traffic methods) to competitors who may copy the site; heavy outreach and buyer qualification needed | Start by approaching competitors in your niche |
| Auctions and aggregators (Flippa, BizBuySell, Centurica feed) | Built-in buyer audience; Flippa approves about 98% of listings; Centurica pulls broker listings into one feed | Lower price, listings sit next to scam or low-quality sites, low multiples; domain, traffic and earnings are public and invite copycats | BizBuySell mainly lists offline businesses |
| Brokers (Empire Flippers, FE International, formerly Flippa Enterprises; Quiet Light Brokerage) | Usually the second-highest price after strategic buyers; done-for-you transfer, buyer vetting and escrow-style fund holding | Fee of 10 to 20% of the sale price; Empire Flippers rejects 30 to 40% of submitted sites | Quiet Light usually handles deals of $500,000 or more |

[L1468.006]

- Doing the sale yourself means no listing or broker fees, but finding and qualifying interested buyers takes a lot of legwork. Sellers without deal experience may find this process difficult. [L1468.007]
- A private sale lets the seller keep 100% of the price, but the seller must find buyers alone. Competitors often request due diligence only to copy the business, so vet any competitor carefully before sharing your books. [L0554.014]
- Brokers help with negotiations involving financing, caveats and time wasters, and they add migration protections for both sides. Brokers also tend to achieve higher prices than sellers on their own, even after their fee. [L0554.015]
- Auction platforms like Flippa offer a hidden-listing option, but using it removes most of the exposure that attracts bids. Sellers who hide their domain and traffic usually get far fewer buyers, so the trade-off between privacy and reach must be weighed deliberately. [L1468.049]
- Online businesses such as Amazon stores or Shopify stores can be sold through EmpireFlippers.com, which handles sales of sites from tens of thousands to hundreds of thousands of dollars. To start, use the sell-your-site button in the top-right corner of the website. (older, 2022) [L0963.152]
- Empire Flippers is an online business brokerage marketplace where owners list online businesses for sale and buyers evaluate them. Listings there require accurate financial records. (older, 2021-22) [L0449.011]
- Empire Flippers is an online business brokerage that buys and sells websites and can help value a store. It was cited as having sold over $560 million in websites. (2025) [L1097.002]
- Empire Flippers listings and brokers such as Empire Flippers handle buying and selling stores. Selling a profitable store, investing the proceeds and starting another store can be a time-saving cycle. [L1515.025]
- Business brokerages list drop shipping stores for sale and usually show revenue and profit figures for each one. (older, 2022) [L0803.099]
- A dropshipping store that earns steady monthly revenue and profit can itself be sold as a whole business on marketplaces such as Flippa. Buyers list the domain together with verified revenue and profit figures, so a profitable store also works as an exit option. [L0549.050]
- Members can sell their online stores to other members. A store for sale is posted in the For Sale section at the top of the channel list, and members browse that section to see available stores. [L0517.006]
- Online stores can be sold through brokers that specialize in internet business sales, such as Empire Flippers. Sale prices in examples of this kind ranged from about $44,000 to $60,000 per store, and the proceeds can then be invested. [L1126.042]
- Doing it yourself: start by approaching competitors in your niche. [L1468.006, L1468.013]

#### Broker process

Steps for selling a dropshipping store through a broker, in order:

1. Get an initial online valuation on the broker website. [L0554.013]
2. Submit the business for vetting, which usually takes 2 to 4 weeks and requires a large amount of information, including checks of the Shopify numbers. [L0554.013]
3. The broker markets the business to its buyer list and may run seller interviews for YouTube. [L0554.013]
4. The seller answers buyer questions and enters negotiations. [L0554.013]
5. The business is transferred to the buyer and the seller receives payout. [L0554.013]

[L0554.013]

General sale process, in order:

1. Obtain an initial valuation of your business. [L0231.018]
2. Decide whether to sell or continue operating. [L0231.018]
3. Complete the vetting process with potential buyers. [L0231.018]
4. Market your business and negotiate terms. [L0231.018]
5. Transfer the business to the new owner and receive payout. [L0231.018]

[L0231.018]

Steps for selling a store or network through a website broker (older, 2020):

1. Wait until the store has been running profitably for at least 12 months and is ideally still growing. [L1277.006]
2. Contact a website broker to schedule a call or start a live chat. [L1277.006]
3. Go through the broker's consultation process. [L1277.006]
4. If the broker sees a good fit and you want to proceed, get listed in the broker's marketplace so the business can be sold. [L1277.006]

[L1277.006]

#### Broker fees and incentives

- Brokers charge 10 to 20% of the sale price as their fee. They reject 30 to 40% of the sites submitted, and rejected sellers get their listing fee refunded. [L1468.008]
- Broker fees are a percentage of the final sale price, so the broker earns more only when the seller earns more. This aligns incentives, but the seller should still check the broker's listing standards and fee terms before signing. [L1468.050]
- The platform's brokers represent the seller and owe the seller a fiduciary duty. They earn fees on the sale price, so sellers must decide their own minimum acceptable price rather than rely on brokers to push for the highest possible offer. [L0553.038]
- Broker fees create a weaker incentive than the seller's own interest in price. On a store that sells for $270,000 instead of a $300,000 list price, the seller loses $30,000 while the broker's fee difference is only about $4,500, roughly 15 percent of that gap. Sellers should therefore decide their own minimum acceptable price before reviewing offers. [L0553.043]
- Brokers that vet listings typically have staff collect the site's information and then have a principal approve or deny each listing. Expect a curated marketplace with rejections rather than automatic acceptance. [L1468.051]

#### Channel eligibility

- Only online businesses are eligible for sale on this platform. Physical-location, retail, offline and real estate-related businesses are not accepted. [L0553.035]
- The sale platform accepts almost any online business model, including non-dropshipping types such as Kindle e-book businesses. It excludes offline businesses, businesses with a physical or local component, and real estate-related businesses. [L0553.045]
- A Shopify store is hard to sell unless it has at least one year of revenue history and roughly 500,000 USD in total revenue. Buyers for such stores were mostly US-based companies, according to the speaker's experience. [L1469.014]
- A store with no real revenue has almost no resale value, and buyers typically want about 12 months of consistent revenue. If a store has no sales, it is usually better to drop it and focus on a new product or store than to try to sell it. [L0660.006]

## Vetting and due diligence

- Brokers' vetting takes 2 to 4 weeks and requires a large amount of information, including checks of the Shopify numbers. [L0554.013]
- Buyers ask for the full financial history, commonly four years of financials. A business without accurate, complete numbers cannot be sold. (older, 2021-22) [L0599.007]
- Basic due diligence reports from third-party services such as Centurica tend to be conservative. Fixing the issues they flag before listing reduces the chance that a buyer uses the report to negotiate the price down. [L1468.052]
- A business with a sustained sharp decline should be considered for sale while it still has value. A buyer pays for something, while a business at zero yields nothing. (older, 2021-22) [L0449.010]
- If a business is declining toward zero, selling it earlier can still recover some value, which is better than selling nothing later. (older, 2021-22) [L0599.008]
- The critical question buyers ask is whether the business will thrive and operate successfully without your direct involvement and daily management. This assessment determines whether they acquire an independent, scalable asset or commit themselves to a full-time job. [L0231.032]
- Buyers reject a store if it lacks a defensible moat, has poor customer experience from China shipping, depends entirely on finding winning Facebook ads, or has no established brand. [L0231.004]
- Store with unexplained dips: each dip should be explained, as stated above. [L0554.006]
- Use the broker's industry report and buyers' numbers to test the store's trend before vetting. [L0554.008]

#### Verifying the numbers before a sale

- Make sure all financial figures in the listing match each other exactly, and that date ranges and metric definitions (sessions or page views) are stated. [L1468.016]
- Prepare the listing materials: earnings records, traffic data, and any video or screencast evidence. [L1468.016]
- Track revenue, costs and owner time separately for each store. [L1468.014]
- Pets niche case: financial data covered from the start of 2016 and earlier, showing a growing business with some dips. [L0554.052]
- Beauty niche case: the accounting was cash-based, and some monthly spikes were caused by when supplier bills were paid. [L0554.053]
- If 30% of your traffic comes from one forum account, buyers will expect that account to be included in the sale. [L1468.009]

#### Vetting of competitors and buyers

- Competitors often request due diligence only to copy the business, so vet any competitor carefully before sharing your books. [L0554.014]
- Buyers who pay high multiples value an online store on trailing 12-month figures. [L0966.001]
- Only 100% online businesses can be sold through the marketplace. [L0554.011]

## Buyer profiles

- Dropshipping store buyers comprise several profiles: cash-rich, time-poor investors seeking passive income; operators who prefer hands-on management; creators focused on building and innovation; cash flow investors prioritizing profitability; and portfolio or fund buyers acquiring multiple businesses for diversification. [L0231.029]
- Typical buyer profiles include a salaried person seeking a lifestyle change with $200,000 to spend on a store earning $8,000 to $9,000 monthly profit; a hands-on buyer looking for a dropshipping store around $40,000 to tweak; and an experienced e-commerce operator who adds stores to existing staff. [L0553.037]
- Buyers of online stores are often people with money but no time, people who want to skip the trial-and-error stage, and institutional or fund buyers. Online assets are compared with index funds and real estate as investments, with higher risk but potentially higher returns. [L0554.010]
- Strategic buyers, usually competitors in the same niche, generally pay the highest price. [L1468.004]
- Some buyers want a passive dividend and do not want to run the site. A broker can place investor capital into a site and have a managing team run it, though this was described as an early test. [L1468.011]
- Private equity typically buys deals of $10 million or more, and the platform handled deals up to about $5 million. Newer micro-funds pooling $10 million to $100 million for small online assets started appearing in the last 12 months. [L0553.036]
- Buyers want to see a business that will thrive without the owner. A store that depends on the owner is a job, not an asset. [L0231.032]
- Dropshipping stores remain attractive to buyers and retain strong valuations despite market concerns that dropshipping is declining. Buyers actively seek dropshipping businesses, particularly those seeking small business capital, and valuations have not fallen drastically. [L0231.031]
- The market outlook for dropshipping is positive, with valuations rising over the previous two years. Recessions may push buyers toward models that need less upfront capital, which can increase demand. [L0554.016]
- Buyers pay for proven monthly net profit. [L0553.001]
- Brokers and strategic buyers as channels for buyer types: [L1468.004, L0553.036]

## Deal terms and payment

- Buyers may pay part of the price at closing and hold back the rest until a contractual obligation is met, such as finishing staff training after the handover. For example, a $300,000 sale could be split into $200,000 paid at closing and $100,000 paid once training is complete. Sellers who train their team before listing avoid this deferred-payment risk. [L0553.041]
- Buyers may hold back part of the payment, for example 50% upfront and 50% over six months, or 70% upfront and 30% after training and handover. This happens when the business depends on the owner's knowledge. [L1468.012]
- The post-sale support a seller offers should match the buyer's experience. Buyers of a small store around $40,000 are often newcomers who need more help, while buyers of stores in the $400,000 to $500,000 range usually have more experience and need less. The support scope is agreed and written down before the deal closes, and extra support can be used as a negotiating point. [L0553.042]
- A non-compete usually applies in the same niche for a set period after the sale. (2024) [L0836.047]
- Check that supplier, affiliate and special pricing terms are clean and can be transferred to a new owner. [L1468.015]
- Sell only once the business is clean: confirm the financials, supplier terms and documentation before terms are negotiated. [L1468.016, L0970.017]

## Handover and SOPs

- Document all processes as standard operating procedures (SOPs). [L1468.016]
- Replace yourself in day-to-day work with VAs, so the business runs without you. [L1468.016]
- A pets store used a customer support virtual assistant for chat, phone calls, emails and Zendesk tickets, and the assistant was willing to continue with the new owner. [L0554.052]
- A beauty store had a full-time virtual assistant handling daily operations, and it had full SOPs, training documents and documentation. [L0554.053]
- Set up a training and site-turnover plan for the new owner. [L1468.016]
- Add at least two alternate suppliers, even if you do not use them. [L1468.016]
- Turn off WHOIS privacy so your name shows as the domain owner. [L1468.016]
- Make sure you control or can grant admin access to analytics. [L1468.014]
- When selling a store, a transition can be handled by informing each supplier that a new owner is taking over, asking them to remove your card from future payments, and putting the new owner's card on file. (2023) [L0970.017]
- A business with documented, transferable systems is worth substantially more than one that depends on the owner. [L1473.035]
- Brands with systems and records are easier to hand over. [L1439.056]
- When a business with several sites is sold, the usual method is an asset sale: the sites, customer lists, historical data and SOPs are sold. The entity itself is then dissolved rather than sold. (older, 2022) [L1092.027]
- Sites in the same niche with overlapping suppliers are valued together, and a buyer will often purchase related sites as a bundle. (older, 2022) [L1092.023]

## Market data and case listings

#### Case listings (sold through a broker)

- Pets niche case: a Shopify store created in August 2014 sold a variety of pet products for several needs of pet owners. It sold for $367K, listed at 33x, and was supported by a Facebook group presence and an email list of about 5,000 emails. Financial data covered from the start of 2016 and earlier, showing a growing business with some dips. Traffic came from 55% paid, 30% organic, 10% direct and 5% other sources. The store worked with several US suppliers, and a customer support virtual assistant handled chat, phone calls, emails and Zendesk tickets, and was willing to continue with the new owner. The store also used paid search. [L0554.052]
- Beauty niche case: a dropshipping store focused on products for salons and massage businesses was listed at $167K at 30x. It had an email list of over 2,000 subscribers that was not monetized but received informational messages and links to blogs. The financial history was long with a strong general trend, though some monthly spikes were caused by when supplier bills were paid, and the accounting was cash-based. It had 18 suppliers, some with exclusive relationships, and about half of traffic came from paid search with some from organic sources. A full-time virtual assistant handled daily operations, and the store had full SOPs, training documents and documentation. [L0554.053]
- AliExpress/apparel case: a store with $8,700 monthly profit, Facebook-only traffic, Chinese shipping and decreasing monthly earnings was listed at $183,000 (21x), took 126 days to sell, and sold for $120,000 (13x). The store was 12 months old, and buyers had the same negative view of AliExpress models. [L0553.029]
- Dropshipping lifestyle case: $5,000 monthly net profit, 18 months old, Google Ads, organic traffic and Facebook retargeting, steady earnings. Listed at $142,000 (28x), sold for $133,000 (26x) in 10 days from listing. [L0553.030]
- Twitter-background website case: see the neglect example above. [L1468.002]
- Home lighting store case: a single home lighting store grew to multi-seven-figure annual revenue and was then sold for a six-figure sum, which freed capital for new stores. After the sale, the operator opened further sites and took partner stakes in other owners' stores. Selling a proven store and reinvesting the proceeds is a route to a portfolio of stores. (older, 2021-22) [L1014.008]
- Early dropshipping store exit: a dropshipping store sold after about one year was priced at roughly one year's salary from a regular job. The first such exit was about 39,500 USD, showing that early store sales can be in the tens of thousands. (older, 2021-22) [L1502.007]
- Building for exit: dropshipping stores can be built with the intent of selling them as an exit. Later stores reached exits in the high six figures, so building, scaling and selling a store is a legitimate business model in its own right. (older, 2021-22) [L1502.008]
- Starting point case: the owner had about 1,000 USD left, no flight ticket home, and was building a store on a borrowed laptop while living in Thailand. The first sale came two months after starting. The store later reached over 1 million USD in total sales with close to 200,000 USD profit. Its web-only theme would have lost sales as shopping moved to phones, so the owner sold the store while it was still healthy and built another store using the same process. [L0826.013]
- The first brand the course group sold achieved a 37 times multiple on monthly net profit. [L1153.013]
- Early AdSense micro-site model: sites earned about 8 USD per month on average, and sites of this kind were sold at about 20 times their net profit. The money from each sale was reinvested into building the next, larger site. [L1359.003]
- Private sale or broker example: sale prices in examples of this kind ranged from about $44,000 to $60,000 per store. [L1126.042]
- Lesson sale of an early dropshipping site netted nearly $200,000 after two years of running it. (older, 2021-22) [L0458.007]

#### Reinvesting the proceeds

- Treat the proceeds from selling a store as investment capital, not spending money. The lesson invested the full proceeds of each sale and kept living costs low so that no sale money was needed for daily life. [L1126.034]
- Selling a profitable store, investing the proceeds into income-producing assets and then starting another store can be a time-saving cycle. Brokers such as Empire Flippers handle buying and selling stores. [L1515.025]
- Selling a proven store and reinvesting the proceeds is a route to a portfolio of stores. After the sale, the operator opened further sites and took partner stakes in other owners' stores. (older, 2021-22) [L1014.008]
- The early AdSense micro-site model reinvested the money from each sale into building the next, larger site. [L1359.003]
- Building a brand that can later be sold makes it easier to build several brands one after another. [L1439.056, L1212.046]
- Some buyers want a passive dividend; investors can place capital into a site run by a managing team. [L1468.011]
- A seller can sell only some of the stores to build a cash reserve and keep the rest. (older, 2020) [L0468.012]

#### Market context

- Brokers' sales data shows dropshipping valuations rising over the two years before the talk. [L0554.016, L0554.008]
- Average store listing multiple in the last 12 months was 27x and the average sold multiple was 24x. [L0553.027]
- The market outlook for dropshipping is positive, and recessions may increase demand for models that need less upfront capital. [L0554.016]

#### Further details

- A store without consistent, established profit or revenue is valued mainly on its assets. Its price is then whatever a buyer is willing to pay. [L1092.025]

To estimate the sale price of a small store with no or low revenue:
1. Open flippa.com.
2. Filter for e-commerce stores with no revenue or low revenue.
3. Note what similar stores sell for and use those prices as a reference for your own store.
[L1092.026]

## Buying an existing store or business

### Build versus buy

- Building a store from scratch is generally preferred to buying one (older advice, 2021). A store with roughly 10,000 to 40,000 in profit can often be rebuilt within a few weeks, so buying it is rarely worth the price. [L0247.002]
- Buying a finished store is usually more expensive than building one, so building and growing the store until it produces income is the more common route (older advice, 2020). [L0967.088]
- Build online stores from scratch rather than buying existing ones (older advice, 2020). [L0852.005]
- Buying an existing online business is generally not recommended for most buyers, because e-commerce businesses are easily replicated. Starting a new store is usually a better use of several hundred thousand dollars than buying one (2024). [L0971.100]
- Buying a profitable store at a high multiple rarely makes sense (older advice, 2019), because a store that sells about one order per day is achievable with focused work and little money. The unit does not state the multiple itself. [L1002.002]
- A better route is to find which niches and stores are for sale, check their numbers, and rebuild a comparable model yourself (older advice, 2019). [L1002.002]

### When buying can make sense

- Buyers purchase an existing drop shipping store to:
  1. shorten their learning curve,
  2. trade money for time,
  3. use momentum the store has already built, or
  4. put a unique advantage of their own to work. [L0858.002]
- For high-ticket dropshipping, the choice depends on your resources (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.001]

| Your position | Usually the better route | Source |
|---|---|---|
| More money than time | Buy an existing store. It saves setup time, but you inherit processes you did not design and must review them carefully. | L0949.001 |
| More time than money | Build from scratch. | L0949.001 |

- Only content sites with established traffic, such as a forum or blog running for around 15 years, are worth buying. Pure e-commerce stores are not (older advice, 2020). [L0852.006]

### Who should not buy yet

- Buyers without prior experience building stores from scratch often overpay and see sales fall soon after purchase, because they cannot judge the quality of the store (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.002]
- Learning to build a store first makes it much easier to evaluate one that is for sale (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.002]

### Verifying figures before paying

1. Verify the seller's figures, supplier relationships, supplier lead times and sales trend. Sellers can present numbers that hide problems, so check everything against the underlying data (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.003]
2. Do your own check of revenue and margins. Many small online sellers keep poor financial records, so the figures they report are often unreliable (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.004]
3. Check the length of the sales history before you agree on a multiple. See the valuation benchmarks below. [L1115.093]

### Valuation benchmarks

| Store or situation | Benchmark | Applies when | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Established profitable store | Around $500,000 or more (older, 2020) | Store with a long, proven profit record | L0852.005 |
| Small, unproven store | Roughly $5,000 to $20,000, risky for beginners (older, 2020) | Little or no track record | L0852.005 |
| Store with roughly 10,000 to 40,000 in profit | Often rebuildable within a few weeks, so buying is rarely worth the price (older, 2021) | Comparing purchase price with build cost | L0247.002 |
| Store with about one year of sales history | About 30 times monthly net profit is typical (2024) | Standard valuation for a store with roughly a year of sales | L1115.093 |
| Store with less than one year of sales history | More risk for the buyer, usually a lower multiple (2024) | Short or unproven sales history | L1115.093 |

- The 2020 price points are older and show only rough orders of magnitude. For a current valuation of a store with known monthly net profit, use the 2024 multiple. [L0852.005, L1115.093]

### Aged domains

- Buying aged domain names is generally not worth the money in drop shipping. Spending large amounts on several aged domains has not paid off (older advice, 2021). [L0853.021]
- An established store with an aged domain, existing backlinks and running traffic lets you pull growth levers quickly (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.005]
- An aged domain with authority does not guarantee fast search rankings, so budget time for SEO even after buying (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.005]
- Both statements apply to different things. An aged domain bought on its own has not been worth the money, while an aged domain that comes with a store that already has backlinks and traffic can speed up growth. [L0853.021, L0949.005]

### Shopify Exchange and broker listings

- Stores listed for sale on Shopify Exchange, as seen around its 2019 launch, were often overpriced, had little or no sales history and carried unrealistic valuations. Check the real revenue history and the asking price critically before buying a store there (older advice, 2019). [L0357.067]
- On a business broker listing for a dropshipping store, the sale includes the domain and all site contents and files. [L0458.062]
- The URL, Google Analytics access and profit-and-loss statement are visible only after you unlock the listing (2021-2022, older). [L0458.062]
- Review the listing carefully before buying (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0458.062]

### Risks for buyers

- You inherit processes you did not design and must review them carefully. [L0949.001]
- You can overpay and watch sales fall, because you cannot judge quality without building stores yourself. [L0949.002]
- The seller's numbers can hide problems. [L0949.003]
- Reported revenue and margins are often unreliable when the seller kept poor records. [L0949.004]
- Small, unproven stores sold for roughly $5,000 to $20,000 are risky for beginners. [L0852.005]
- An aged domain does not guarantee fast rankings. [L0949.005]
- E-commerce businesses are easily replicated, which weakens the case for paying for one. [L0971.100]

### Learning due diligence

- The podcast Acquisitions Anonymous has its host vet two businesses a week as if buying them. It is a useful way to learn due diligence before purchasing an online store (older advice, 2021-2022). [L0949.064]

## Other details

### Quick wins on an established store

- Apply these steps in order to a store that already trades. [L0575.028]
  1. Add abandoned cart emails. [L0575.028]
  2. Add "buy" and "online" to product title tags. [L0575.028]
  3. Make sure Google Shopping feed monitoring is set up daily, and split product types into separate campaigns. [L0575.028]
  4. Negotiate provider discounts (Shopify, payment processor, PayPal) by phone, and review annual billing. [L0575.028]
  5. Rewrite meta descriptions using the rules: capitals, unique per page, sales-letter style, rich snippets, and a phone number where relevant. [L0575.028]
  6. Disable pagination. [L0575.028]
  7. Set up uptime monitoring. [L0575.028]
  8. Validate Google Analytics e-commerce tracking, and remove PayPal Express buttons. [L0575.028]
- A store doing about $1 million in revenue applied three of these quick wins (titles, Google Shopping structure and meta descriptions among them) and added about $200,000 in revenue within three months. [L0575.029]
- A separate store cut Shopify costs by $100 per month with one phone call, saving over $1,000 per year. [L0575.029]

### Return fees

- Return fees spread first in the US, then in Australia, and more slowly into the UK and EU. Large fashion retailers such as ASOS and Zara now charge them. [L0955.059]
- Expect fee-based return policies to become the norm in each market, at a different speed in each. [L0955.059]

### Return on investment

- Example: a $2,000 retreat investment (flights, hotel and ticket combined) should generate $20,000 in annual profit through applied strategies, a 10x return. [L0286.003]

### Diagnosing a store that does not make money

- Older advice (2019): if a store is not making money, find the cause. It is either traffic quality and conversion, or product and supplier quality. [L1068.013]
- Spending more money on the same setup does not fix it. [L1068.013]
- Do not close a store before the root cause has been checked. [L1068.013]

### Competitive position

- Older advice (2019): the business is relationship-based, which is a real barrier against copycats. [L1086.051]
- Supplier relationships, optimized product pages, traffic setup and ad accounts all take work. That work is what separates the stores that win. [L1086.051]

### Platforms and emerging channels

- Older data (late 2022): Meta's VR headset platform had about 200,000 monthly active users against a 500,000 target. [L1092.109]
- VR 3D shopping was therefore not a planning factor for online stores in the coming few years. [L1092.109]

### Tools

- PayHip is a platform for selling digital products such as PDFs. [L1216.027]
- Setup: add the product, set up checkout, and customize the store design. It then generates a simple sales link. [L1216.027]
- Its checkout is a one-page checkout. [L1216.027]
